Approximation of non-linear SPDEs with additive noise via weighted interacting particles systems: the stochastic Mckean-Vlasov equation
L. Angeli (1), D. Crisan (2), M. Kolodziejczyk (3), M. Ottobre (4)

Abstract
. This paper is devoted to the problem of approximating non-linear Stochastic Partial Differential Equations (SPDEs) via interacting particle systems. In particular, we consider the Stochastic McKean-Vlasov equation , which is the McKean-Vlasov (MKV) PDE (also known in other contexts as the porous media equation), perturbed by additive trace class noise. As is well-known, the MKV PDE can be obtained as mean field limit of the empirical measure of a stochastic system of interacting particles, where particles are subject to independent sources of noise. If the particles are subject to the same noise (often referred to as common noise ), as opposed to independent sources of noise, the limit of the associated empirical measure is no longer deterministic, and it is described by an SPDE. The SPDE emerging in this context can be viewed as a stochastic perturbation of the MKV PDE, where the noise structure is multiplicative and in gradient form. There is now a natural question, which is the one we consider and answer in this paper: can we obtain the SMKV equation, i.e. additive perturbations of the MKV PDE, as limit of interacting particle systems? It turns out that, in order to obtain the SMKV equation, one needs to study weighted empirical measures of particles, where the particles evolve according to a system of SDEs with independent noise, while the weights are time evolving and subject to common noise. Besides achieving the immediate goal of constructing a system of interacting particles which converges to the SMKV equation, the approach used in this work is potentially suggestive of ways to construct interacting particle systems to approximate other non-linear SPDEs, such as (variations of) Dean-Kawasaki, Keller-Segel-Dean-Kawasaki in particular. Furthermore, dealing with additive noise structures required new ideas of proof, and these ideas have the potential of being easily adapted to more general sources of noise, such as jump processes. The work of this manuscript therefore complements and contributes to various streams of literature, in particular: i) much attention in the community is currently devoted to obtaining SPDEs as scaling limits of appropriate dynamics; this paper contributes to a complementary stream, which is devoted to obtaining representaions of SPDE through limits of empirical measures of interacting particle systems; ii) since the literature on limits of weighted empirical measures is often constrained to the case of static (random or deterministic) weights, this paper contributes to further expanding this line of research to the case of time-evolving weights. Keywords. Stochastic Partial Differential Equations, Interacting Particle Systems, Stochastic McKean-Vlasov Equation, Particle Approximations to Stochastic Partial Differential Equations. AMS Subject Classification. 35Q83, 35Q70, 60H15, 35R60, 65C35, 82M60.



1.
Introduction

This paper is concerned with the problem of obtaining non-linear Stochastic Partial Differential equations (SPDEs) with
additive
noise as limits of appropriate interacting particle systems.
More precisely, we consider nonlinear McKean-Vlasov Partial differential Equations (PDEs) with stochastic or deterministic forcing; that is, evolutions of the following type

(1.1)

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}(x)=\partial_{xx}\nu_{t}(x)+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{\prime}(x)+(F^{\prime}\ast\nu_{t})(x))\nu_{t}(x)\right]+\partial_{t}\,\mathcal{U}_{t}

where the unknown
\nu=\nu_{t}(x)
is a real-valued function,
1
1

1

Throughout this
paper, for any quantity, say
Y
, that depends on time, we use interchangeably the notation
Y_{t}
or

Y(t)
to denote time-dependence and we use the notation
Y_{\cdot}
or
t\mapsto Y_{t}
when we want to refer to the whole path.

\nu:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, with
\mathbb{T}
the one dimensional torus of length
2\pi
;
V
and
F
are given smooth functions,
V,F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
, usually referred to as the
environmental
and
inter-particle
potentials, respectively (below the reason for this nomenclature);
′
denotes derivative with respect to the spatial variable and
\mathcal{U}
is a forcing term, which could depend on time only, or on both space and time. We will consider three cases:
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\mathcal{U}(t,x)=q(x)Y_{t}
where
q
is a smooth function on the torus and
Y_{t}
is a
\alpha
-Hölder continuous time-dependent function,
Y_{\cdot}\in C^{\alpha}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
;
2
2

2

We recall that
C^{\alpha}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
is the space of real valued paths
f
on the interval
[0,T]
,
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto f(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, for which there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|f(t)-f(s)|\leq C|t-s|^{\alpha}
, for every
t,s\in[0,T]
.

\mathcal{U}_{t}=q(x)w_{t}
where
w_{t}
is a one-dimensional standard Brownian motion (and then, by easy extension, a finite sum of terms of this form);
\mathcal{U}
is a (weighted) infinite sum of Brownian motions, namely
\mathcal{U}=W(t,x)
, where
W
is trace class Wiener noise,

(1.2)

W(t,x):=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(x)w^{z}_{t}\,,

where
\{e_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
,
\{\lambda_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a (non-identically zero) square-summable sequence of non-negative real numbers and the
w_{t}^{z}
’s are standard one-dimensional independent Brownian motions. To be more explicit, we will consider the following three evolutions:

(1.3)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho_{t}(x)=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}(x)+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}(x)+(F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t})(x))\rho_{t}(x)\right]+q(x)\,\partial_{t}Y_{t},\quad t\in(0,T),\,x\in\mathbb{T},\\
&\rho|_{t=0}=\rho_{0}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}\,,\end{cases}

where
Y_{\cdot}
is a
\alpha
- Hölder path,

(1.4)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)=\partial_{xx}u_{t}(x)+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}(x)+(F^{{}^{\prime}}*u_{t})(x))u_{t}(x)\right]+q(x)\,\partial_{t}w_{t},\quad t\in(0,T),x\in\mathbb{T},\\
&u|_{t=0}=u_{0}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}\,,\end{cases}

where
w_{t}
is a one-dimensional real-valued standard Brownian motion, and finally

(1.5)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}v_{t}(x)=\partial_{xx}v_{t}(x)+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}(x)+(F^{{}^{\prime}}*v_{t})(x))v_{t}(x)\right]+\,\partial_{t}W_{t},\quad t\in(0,T),x\in\mathbb{T}\\
&v|_{t=0}=v_{0}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}\,,\end{cases}

where the trace class noise
W
is as in (
1.2
). In the first case, when
\mathcal{U}=q(x)Y_{t}
, the resulting equation (
1.3
) is deterministic - it is simply a PDE with a deterministic forcing term, so the unknown
\rho=\rho(t,x):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a deterministic function of time and space. In the second and third case, (
1.4
) and (
1.5
), respectively, the resulting evolutions are SPDEs. The unknowns
u=u(t,x):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
v=v(t,x):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are random functions of space and time and, as customary, in the notation we omit the dependence of
u
and
v
on the realization
\omega
in the underlying probability space.

Well-posedness and long-time behaviour of (
1.5
) have been investigated in
[
2
]
. The well-posedness of (
1.5
) when
W
is cylindrical Wiener noise is still an open problem, and this is the main reason why in this paper we consider trace class noise only. The well-posedness of (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) can be obtained similarly to the well-posedness of (
1.5
), see Section
2
.

The purpose of this paper is to find particle approximations to the above dynamics, i.e. to find appropriate particle systems which converge, in a sense that we will specify below, to the solution of (
1.1
). To explain what spurred our interest in evolutions of type (
1.1
) (which may a priori seem simpler than their counterparts with multiplicative noise), let us start by introducing the following system of
N
interacting particles

(1.6)

dX_{t}^{i,N}=\left[-V^{\prime}(X_{t}^{i,N})+\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}F^{\prime}(X_{t}^{j,N}-X_{t}^{i,N})\right]dt+\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}^{i},

where, for every
i=1\dots N
,
X_{t}^{i,N}\in\mathbb{T}
represents the position of the
i-
th particle at time
t>0
. The Interacting particle system (IPS) (
1.6
) has been extensively studied in the literature, as a model for collective dynamics and consensus formation, see e.g.
[
18
,
32
,
22
,
9
,
5
]
and references therein. The potential
F
describes the interaction between particles, (
inter-particle potential
), while
V
, which is the same for each particle (it does not depend on
i
) is commonly used to describe environmental effects (
environmental potential
); the
\beta_{t}^{i}
’s are independent one-dimensional standard Brownian motions. When the number
N
of particles in the system is large, it is natural to look for a macroscopic (coarse-grained) description of system (
1.6
). To do so, according to an established methodology in statistical mechanics, one considers the
empirical measure

h_{t}^{N}
associated to the particle system (
1.6
),
h^{N}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\delta_{X_{t}^{i,N}}
, which is, for each
t>0
, a random probability measure. Then, in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
(generally referred to as
mean field limit
),
h_{t}^{N}
converges weakly in
C([0,T];\mathbb{R})
(but results on infinite time horizons do hold true in certain cases as well, see e.g.
[
23
,
33
,
3
]
and references therein) to the solution of the following non-linear (and non-local) PDE

(1.7)

\partial_{t}h_{t}(x)=\sigma\partial_{xx}h_{t}(x)+\partial_{x}\left[\left(V^{\prime}(x)+(F^{\prime}\ast h_{t})(x)\right)h_{t}(x)\right]\,,

for the unknown
h=h(t,x):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
provided this is true for the corresponding initial data, i.e provided
h_{0}^{N}
converges to

h_{0}
, see
[
28
,
40
]
. In short, we say that the PDE (
1.7
) is obtained as limit of the IPS (
1.6
), meaning that the empirical measure
h^{N}
associated to (
1.6
) converges to the solution
h
of the PDE (
1.7
). The convergence of
h^{N}
to
h
means that the particle system can be used as an approximation of the limiting PDE. Note that while the particle system (
1.6
) is stochastic, the limit (
1.7
) is deterministic, fact that can be viewed as a manifestation of the law of large numbers. Another, more pathwise perspective on this is the following: instead of looking at the convergence of the empirical measure, one can show that, as

N\rightarrow\infty
, the particles become independent (
propagation of chaos
) and, in the
limit, the motion of each of them is described by the following Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE)

dX_{t}=-\left(V^{\prime}(X_{t})+\int_{\mathbb{T}}F^{\prime}(y-X_{t})h_{t}(dy)\right)dt+\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}\,,\quad X_{t}\in\mathbb{T}\,,

where
\beta_{t}
is a one-dimensional standard Brownian motion and
h_{t}
is the law of
X_{t}
at time
t
, so that the above evolution is non-linear in the sense that the process depends on its own law, i.e. it is
non-linear in the sense of McKean
. The law
h_{t}
of
X_{t}
can then be shown to be the solution to the PDE (
1.7
). For later purposes we note that the PDE (
1.7
) preserves mass, i.e. (the solution is positive if started from a positive initial datum) and the value of the integral
\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{t}(x)\,dx
is constant in time. This is compatible with the fact that during the evolution (
1.6
) the number of particles is preserved.

Equation (
1.1
) is obtained by adding a forcing term to the PDE (
1.7
); for this reason, when the forcing
\mathcal{U}
is either of the form
q(x)w_{t}
or a trace class Wiener noise, we refer to the resulting equations, equations (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) respectively, (the latter being the one that initially motivated this work) as to
McKean-Vlasov SPDEs
or, more accurately, as
Stochastic McKean-Vlasov equations
(SMKV).
4
4

4

The former name could be misleading so we clarify that the solution to (
1.5
), seen as a function-space-valued process, is not an infinite dimensional McKean-Vlasov SDE, as the process does not depend on its own law. The investigation of infinite-dimensional McKean-Vlasov SDEs has been recently tackled in
[
35
,
34
]
, see
[
2
]
for a more thorough discussion on this.
We mention in passing that our interest in the evolution (
1.5
) started when considering problems in infinite dimensional sampling (which are unrelated to the work of this paper, but we mention nonetheless for context). The flavour of this initial motivation is not dissimilar to the one in the very recent study
[
21
]
, which treats related types of equations. After the seminal paper
[
17
]
, stochastically perturbed McKean-Vlasov dynamics (though not necessarily with additive noise) have also been considered in
[
7
,
8
,
38
,
43
,
20
]
(to mention just a few works on the matter and without claim to completeness of references) as such equations naturally arise in the context of mean field games or, potentially, as models in mathematical biology.

If one is interested, as we are, in obtaining, in the limit, a stochastic PDE, a useful framework to consider is the one of IPSs subject to
common noise
, which is a relatively simple variation of the mean field paradigm we have just summerised. A simple instance of the common noise setting can be seen by considering the following IPS

(1.8)

dX_{t}^{i,N}=-V^{\prime}(X_{t}^{i,N})dt+\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}F^{\prime}(X_{t}^{j,N}-X_{t}^{i,N})dt+\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}^{i}+\sqrt{2\tilde{\sigma}}dw_{t},

where, crucially, the one-dimensional Brownian noise
w_{t}
is
the same
for each particle (i.e. it is the ‘common noise’), and
\tilde{\sigma}>0
.
Because the same noise
w_{t}
acts on all the particles, the limit of the particle system (
1.8
) is no longer deterministic, and it is stochastic instead
[
40
,
8
]
. In particular, the limit of the empirical measure associated with (
1.8
) is given by the following SPDE

(1.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h_{t}(x)=\partial_{x}\left[\left(V^{\prime}(x)+(F^{\prime}\ast h_{t})(x)\right)h_{t}(x)+(\sigma+\tilde{\sigma})\partial_{x}h_{t}(x)\right]-\sqrt{2\tilde{\sigma}}(\partial_{x}h_{t})dw_{t}\,.

The limiting equation (
1.9
) is in this case interpreted as the conditional law of the following McKean-Vlasov evolution

dx_{t}=-\left(V^{\prime}(x_{t})+\int_{\mathbb{T}}F^{\prime}(y-x_{t})h_{t}(dy)\right)dt+\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}+\sqrt{2\tilde{\sigma}}dw_{t}\,,\quad X_{t}\in\mathbb{T}\,;

that is, in the above
h_{t}
is the conditional law of
x_{t}
, conditional on the path
\{w_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
; in short
h_{t}=\mathcal{L}(x_{t}|w)
, see
[
40
,
8
]
. In the common noise setting the common noise is treated as a given forcing or, to put it differently, as an extra, given, time-dependent drift, hence the conditioning. From a modelling perspective, the common noise is often use to describe ‘shocks’ to the system, which affect each particle, see e.g.
[
41
,
8
]
.

The common noise setting will be conceptually useful to us but the dynamics (
1.9
) is not what we wanted to obtain.
For the purposes of this discussion, the main difference between (
1.5
) (or more, generally, (
1.1
)) and (
1.9
) is that in the former equation noise acts additively on the solution (the forcing
\mathcal{U}_{t}
does not depend on the solution itself), while in the latter noise is multiplicative; moreover, (
1.9
) has transport (gradient) structure, while (
1.1
) does not - fact that is the source of many complications. Notice indeed that both the drift (the PDE part) and the diffusion term of (
1.9
) are in gradient form. Hence, by integration by parts, one can formally see that
the gradient structure of (
1.9
) implies that mass is preserved along the solution of (
1.9
); that is, if
h
is the solution of (
1.9
), then

\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{t}(x)\,dx=C,
almost surely,
where
C
is a constant independent of time. Notice again that also in the IPS (
1.8
) the number of particles is conserved (particles are neither killed nor reweighted).

However equation (
1.1
)
does not
in general preserve mass and it does not preserve
positivity either, so one
cannot expect
to obtain (
1.1
) (or any of its incarnations (
1.3
), (
1.4
), (
1.5
)) as limit of
empirical (probability) measures, similarly to what we have described above for
(
1.6
)/(
1.8
) and their corresponding limit (
1.7
)/(
1.9
) (where no killing/reweighting of
particles takes place). Intuitively, one expects that, in order to obtain (
1.1
), particles will need to be reweighted in some way.

In this paper we will indeed show that equations of the form (
1.1
) can be obtained as
limits of a weighted interacting particle system. The weights we will use are not “static weights" (i.e. fixed, independent of time); on the contrary, the evolution of the weights is coupled to the evolution of the particles and it is described by a system of stochastic differential equations.
In the particle-weight system that we will design the particles are driven by independent sources of noise, while the evolution of the weights is subject to common forcing (common noise in the stochastic case of (
1.4
) and (
1.5
)).
In Subsection
1.1
below we
give a heuristic argument to explain how the particle-weight system which converges to (
1.5
) is constructed, and provide a slightly simplified, informal version of the particle-weight system we will consider, see (
1.10
)-(
1.11
). In Section
2
we present full results.

The inspiration to construct our particle-weight system comes from
[
39
,
15
]
(see also references therein), which are devoted to finding particle representations of non-linear SPDEs (we will explain the difference between particle
representation
and particle
approximation
in Note
1.2
). The class of non-linear SPDEs considered in
[
39
,
15
]
includes non-linearities of Allen-Cahn or Fisher KPP type and moreover
[
39
]
treats multiplicative equations only. In particular, the class of SPDEs considered in
[
39
,
15
]
does not include equations of the type (
1.1
). Coping with equations of type (
1.1
) requires new ideas, and fundamentally different approaches in the proofs. We will make a more thorough
comparison with
[
39
,
15
]
in Note
1.2
and explain the full strategy of proof in Section
2.1
, here we give a flavour.

Our interest was initially in studying the Stochastic McKean-Vlasov equation i.e. equation (
1.5
); one then quickly realises that, from the point of view of deriving particle approximations, the infinite dimensional nature of the noise in (
1.5
) does not pose any particular difficulty, so that it is convenient ( to set up proofs, to simplify notation and for understanding) to start by considering equation (
1.4
), which is forced by a simple one dimensional Brownian Motion, and deriving particle approximations to such an evolution. Our strategy
of proof strongly relies on fixing a realization of the Brownian path
w_{t}
driving (
1.4
) and on viewing (
1.4
) as a specific instance of (
1.1
), when the forcing in (
1.1
) is given by such a realization. Because of the Hölder regularity of Brownian motion, with this approach one is led to considering PDEs of the form (
1.3
), where the forcing
Y_{t}
has the same Hölder continuity of Brownian motion, but it is fixed deterministically. Since
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions can be approximated via piece-wise smooth functions, all our results are obtained by considering a sequence
\{Y^{\kappa}_{t}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
of
piecewise smooth, time-dependent deterministic functions which converge (in appropriate sense, see Section

2
) to
Y_{t}
. We first prove all the statements of interest when
(
1.1
) is driven by a piecewise smooth forcing, i.e.
\mathcal{U}=q(x)Y^{\kappa}_{t}

and then,
at the appropriate point in the proof
, we take the limit in
\kappa
. Some of the technical advantags of doing all proofs by “fixing the driving path"
are explained as we move along the proof, in Note
2.8
, Note
2.13
and Note
6.9
. In few words, if the driving force is smooth then no Itô second order correction terms arise from the chain rule. In our case these terms would be quite complicated to handle. An alternative, intuitive way to explain why this approach can succeed comes from the observation that since the equations
we are studying are subject to additive noise, their Itô and Stratonovich interpretation coincide; hence, the
reason why this approach works can then be seen in the general framework of Wong-Zakai approximation theory
[
48
,
37
]
. Moreover, since the Brownian path
w_{t}
in (
1.4
) (similarly for
W
in (
1.5
)) will be precisely the common noise acting on the weights of our particle-weight system (see (
1.11
) and (
1.12
)), this perspective is coherent with the fact that, when dealing with common noise (see comments after equation (
1.9
)), it is customary to carry out all proofs by ‘conditioning on the forcing common noise’. Related to this, another, perhaps more pragmatic way of seeing the approach of fixing the noise, is the following: we will construct (
1.3
) as (weighted) marginal of a certain joint distribution; of course, there are many joint distributions which would give the same marginal. From this perspective, fixing the noise corresponds to choosing a convenient joint distribution - convenient for the purposes of simplifying the proof, see Note
2.8
.

However
these are just intuitive explanations. Indeed,
complications come from the fact that, while the equation in which we are interested is subject to additive noise,
other quantities of interest in the proof satisfy equations which are driven by multiplicative noise (see Section

2
). So taking the limit of the regularization parameter at the appropriate
moment is crucial, to avoid dealing with complicated second order Stratonovich correction terms.
Finally, our proofs do not require any rough path theory; nonetheless the paper
[
12
]
has been of great inspiration - the methods of proof we use here are very different, but the inspiration of trying to simplify proofs by “fixing the driving path" has certainly come from
[
12
]
.

We do not claim that this is the only possible approach of proof, but we argue for its simplicity.
The approach of fixing the path has two perks: on the one hand it is extremely helpful in overcoming
a number of technical hurdles; on the other hand it allows in principle
to produce results for SPDEs with many types of noise - whichever noise has paths that can be
approximated with time-dependent paths of appropriate smoothness, as long as the corresponding
equation (
1.1
) is well posed. We indeed believe that the same approach would work if the forcing was fractional Brownian motion or a jump process, as e.g. in
[
46
]
.

Our results contribute to broadening the literature on limits of weighted empirical measures as well. The literature on this topic is large and a full review is out of the scope of this paper, so we mention only some works, with no claim to completeness of reference. Beyond the mean field regime (where all weights are fixed and equal to one) interest has been attracted by the case in which the weights are fixed, deterministic or random, as e.g. in the case of Erdös graphs (see
[
13
]
, which contains a very thorough review). Motivated by applications e.g. to mathematical biology, epidemiology or opinion formation
[
29
,
30
]
, time-evolving weights (representing an evolving network of connections), possibly coupled to the particles’ evolution, have also been investigated, see
[
45
,
3
]
and references therein.
However, aside from the already mentioned works
[
39
,
15
]
, we are not aware of other references where the weights are coupled to the particles and subject to common noise.

A final note on connection with broader literature: as is well known, much attention in the community is currently
devoted to
deriving SPDEs as
scaling limits
of appropriate processes - we refer the reader to
[
4
]
and references therein for a nice and accessible review. In this case one seeks to obtain the limiting equation via appropriate rescaling of space and time. One can also ask the question of whether it is possible to obtain SPDEs as limits of interacting particle systems, in the sense we have explained in this introduction. This is the question that the works
[
39
,
15
]
, see also references therein, and the
present paper are trying to address.

In this context we point out that the framework of this work could be used to inspire particle approximations to (versions of) the Dean-Kawasaki equation, see e.g.
[
25
,
14
,
19
]
or
[
44
]
, on the Keller-Segel-Dean-Kawasaki equation. This will be object of future work.

In Subsection
1.1
below we give a heuristic argument for the choice of particle-weight system. Precise statements of main results, strategy of proof and organization of the paper are described in Section
2
.

1.1.
Informal statement of main result.

We start by giving an ansatz for the particle-weight system that converges to (
1.5
). We then state an informal version of the main result of this paper and provide a heuristic calculation to show why the result is expected to hold.

Consider the following system,

(1.10)

\displaystyle dX^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,

\displaystyle=\,-V^{\prime}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})dt-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}A^{j,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,F^{\prime}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon}-X_{t}^{j,N,\varepsilon})\,dt+\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}^{i}

(1.11)

\displaystyle dA^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})dw_{t}^{z}}{\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}-X^{j,N,\varepsilon}_{t})}\,,

where
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and, for each
i
,
(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t},A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. One should think of
X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon}
as representing the position of the
i
-th particle at time
t
, and
A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}
as representing a weight. The
\beta_{t}^{i}
’s are one-dimensional Brownian motions, independent of each other and of the one-dimensional Brownian motions
\{w_{t}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
; the
\lambda_{z}
’s and
e_{z}
’s are as in (
1.2
). The functions
\Phi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are ‘mollifiers’, i.e. they are a family (indexed by
\varepsilon>0
) of smooth functions, converging to the Dirac delta at zero, strictly positive for each
\varepsilon>0
fixed, and normalised so that
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)dx=1
, the role of which is explained in few lines. The particle-weight system (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) will be used to obtain (
1.5
), see Theorem
1.1
below.

The reader who is unfamiliar with infinite dimentional noise may find it simpler to focus on equation (
1.4
) rather than on (
1.5
). This is not an oversimplification and in fact the core of the problem we will discuss is not related to using infinite dimensional noise, but rather to the fact that the noise we consider
is additive and to the specific nature of the nonlinearity we are trying to obtain. Note indeed that equation (
1.4
)
is substantially a specific instance of (
1.5
) when
\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{2\pi}
,
e_{0}(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}
and
\lambda_{z}=0
for every
z\neq 0
. So, to obtain the SPDE (
1.4
) instead of (
1.5
) just consider (
1.10
) coupled to the weights
\tilde{A}^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}
, evolving according to

(1.12)

d\tilde{A}^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,=\,\frac{q(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})}{\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}-X^{j,N,\varepsilon}_{t})}dw_{t}\,,

rather than to the weights (
1.11
). In this simpler case one can more directly see that, while the particles
X_{t}^{i}
are subject to independent sources of noise (the
\beta_{t}^{i}
’s), the weights
A_{t}^{i}
are subject to the
same
noise (the Brownian
w_{t}
is the same for every
i
).

From now on we go back to referring to the system (
1.10
)-(
1.11
). Let us introduce the
weighted empirical measure
v^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}
defined as

(1.13)

v^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}(dx):=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot\delta_{X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}}(dx)\,,

where the particles
X^{i,N,\varepsilon}
and the weights
A^{i,N,\varepsilon}
are as in (
1.10
)-(
1.11
). Then the following holds.

Theorem 1.1

(Informal Statement of main result)
.

Let
v_{t}
be the solution of (
1.5
) and
v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}
as in (
1.13
). Then the weighted empirical measure
v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}

converges weakly, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and
N\rightarrow\infty
, to the solution of (
1.5
); that is,

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}-v_{t},f\rangle=0\,,a.s.

for every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and where in the above
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes duality pairing between measures and functions.

The complete statement of this theorem and the strategy of proof are contained in Section
2
, see Theorem
2.6
. To explain the presence of the mollifier
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.11
), note that the term at the denominator of (
1.11
) can be written as

(1.14)

\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}-X^{j,N,\varepsilon}_{t})=(\Phi_{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon})(X^{i,N,\varepsilon})

where

(1.15)

\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(dx):=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}}(dx)\,;

that is,
\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}
is the empirical measure associated with the particle system
\{X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\}_{i=1}^{N}
. From the heuristic calculation that we will do in the next section (see (
1.19
) and comments afterwards) it will become clear that, in principle, one would like to consider (
1.10
) coupled to the following weights

dA_{t}^{i,N}=\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(X_{t}^{i,N})dw_{t}^{z}}{\zeta_{t}^{N}(X_{t}^{i,N})}\,,

instead of (
1.11
). However, from a technical point of view, it is not clear how to make sense of the above object, because of the presence of the empirical measure at the denominator. This is where the mollification comes into play.

We emphasize that the SPDE (
1.5
) is not obtained as limit of the empirical measure
\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}
(which is a random probability measure) associated to the particle system, but rather as limit of the weighted empirical measure
v^{N,\varepsilon}
(
1.13
) (which is a random
signed
measure).
Let us now come to provide a heuristic argument to explain why
v^{N,\varepsilon}
converges to the solution of the SPDE (
1.5
).

1.2.
Heuristics

In this section we take the particle-weight system (
1.10
)-(
1.11
)
as an ansatz; given this ansatz, we provide an informal argument to show that, assuming
v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}
converges, as
N\rightarrow\infty
and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, to some limit
v_{t}
, such a limit is expected to be the solution of the SPDE (
1.5
). One can also define a weighted
empirical measure using the particle-weight system (
1.10
)-(
1.12
) and
such a measure would converge to the solution of (
1.4
) (to follow the calculation
below in the latter simpler case just set
\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{2\pi}
,
\lambda_{z}=0
for every
z\neq 0
and take
e_{0}=(2\pi)^{-1/2}
).

By applying Itô’s formula to the process
\{A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,f(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\}_{t\geq 0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle d\left(A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,f(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\right)\,

\displaystyle=\,A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot f^{\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,dX^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,+\sigma\,A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot f^{\prime\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,dt\,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\,+\,f(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,dA^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}+\,f^{\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,dA^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,dX^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,.

Formally, since the Brownian motions
\beta_{t}^{i}
’s are independent of the
w_{t}^{z}
’s, the last addend in the above vanishes. As for the first addend, note that the displacement
dX_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon}
in (
1.10
) can be rewritten, using (
1.13
), as

dX^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,=\,-[V^{\prime}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})+(F^{\prime}\ast v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon})(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})]dt+\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}^{i}\,.

Similarly, from (
1.11
) and (
1.14
), the displacement
dA_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon}
appearing in the third addend on the right hand side (RHS) of (
1.16
) can be rewritten as

dA_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon}=\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})dw_{t}^{z}}{(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon})(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})}\,.

Using these observations, we then get

\displaystyle d\left(A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,f(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=-A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot\left[V^{\prime}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})+(F^{\prime}\ast v^{N,\varepsilon}_{t})(X^{i,N}_{t})\right]f^{\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,dt

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2}\,A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}f^{\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,d\beta_{t}^{i}+\sigma\,A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot f^{\prime\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,dt

(1.17)

\displaystyle+\,f(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\cdot\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})dw_{t}^{z}}{(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon})(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})}\,.

Since

\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}f(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon})=\langle f,v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}\rangle,

taking sums over
i=1,\dots,N
on both sides of (
1.17
) and dividing by
N
, we obtain

\displaystyle d\langle f,\,v^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-V^{\prime}\,f^{\prime}-(F^{\prime}\ast v^{N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,f^{\prime}+\sigma f^{\prime\prime},\,v^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}\rangle\,dt+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}\,f^{\prime}(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})\,d\beta_{t}^{i}

(1.18)

\displaystyle+\left\langle f\,\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}dw_{t}^{z}}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}},\,\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\ ,

where
v^{N,\varepsilon}
is the weighted empirical measure (
1.13
). Suppose now that, as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}
and
v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}
converge to some functions (or, more generally, measures)
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}
and
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, respectively. Suppose also that there exist functions
\zeta_{t}
and
v_{t}
such that
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\zeta_{t}
and
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow v_{t}
(in an appropriate sense), as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Then, formally passing to the limits
N\rightarrow\infty,\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
in each of the terms in (
1.18
) one can see that
v_{t}
will satisfy (
1.5
), in weak sense. Indeed, the martingale term (the second addend on the RHS of (
1.18
)) is a mean-zero term with quadratic variation of order
N^{-1}
and will therefore disappear in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
. As for the last term, which is the most delicate, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle f\,\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}dw_{t}^{z}}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}},\,\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left\langle f\,\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}dw_{t}^{z}}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta^{\varepsilon}_{t}},\,\zeta^{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

(1.19)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left\langle f\,\frac{\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}dw_{t}^{z}}{\zeta_{t}},\,\zeta_{t}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)\partial_{t}W(t,x)\,dx\,,

provided that
\zeta_{t}
has strictly positive density.
This shows that the weights
A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t}
are designed in such a way that, in the limit, the dependence on the density
\zeta_{t}
associated to the particle system ‘cancels’ thanks to the simplification in the last step of the above, so that the resulting limiting noise is additive.

Clearly, many of the technical difficulties in this paper arise from needing estimates from below, which are uniform in the appropriate parameter, on the denominator in (
1.11
). Comments on how these have been addressed can be found in Note
5.5
(and the associated Lemma
5.4
). Informally, one can see that having these lower bounds allows one to rigorously perform the ’cancellation’ in the above. The part of the proof where this ‘cancellation’ is made precise is in Section
6
, see Note
6.9
.

Since the IPS (
1.6
) converges to (
1.7
),
one might wonder why we are not using the particle-weight system (
1.6
)-(
1.11
) in order to obtain (
1.5
), rather than the system (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) (note that in the former case the particles would be independent of the weights). As one can see by applying an analogous heuristic argument to the one shown above, if we were to use the particle-weight system (
1.6
)-(
1.11
), then the corresponding weighted empirical measure
v_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}
would converge to the following SPDE

(1.20)

\partial_{t}v_{t}=\partial_{xx}v_{t}+\partial_{x}\left[V^{{}^{\prime}}v_{t}+(F^{{}^{\prime}}*\zeta_{t})v_{t}\right]+\partial_{t}W_{t},

where
\zeta_{t}
again is the limit (assuming it exists) of the empirical measure of the particles. In this case
\zeta_{t}
would solve the PDE (
1.7
), the evolution of which is completely decoupled from the evolution (
1.20
).
Hence, the limiting SPDE (
1.20
) would be linear; in particular, we would not obtain the desired non-linearity.

Finally, if the equation for the particles was given by (
1.10
) and the equation for the weights was not divided by
\Phi^{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta_{t}^{N}
, i.e. if the weights were chosen to evolve according to

dA_{t}^{i,N}={\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(X_{t}^{i,N})dw_{t}^{z}}\,,

from (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) it is easy to see that the limiting equation would be

(1.21)

\partial_{t}v_{t}=\partial_{xx}v_{t}+\partial_{x}\left[V^{{}^{\prime}}v_{t}+(F^{{}^{\prime}}*v_{t})v_{t}\right]+\zeta_{t}\partial_{t}W_{t}\,.

This time however the evolution for
\zeta_{t}
and
v_{t}
would be coupled, so the noise in the above is multiplicative, which is not what we want.

The statement in Theorem
1.1
is informal. In particular, both the statements of the theorem and the heuristics that we have just presented contain only two limits, the many particle limit and the
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
limit. In reality we will need to take four limits, the two additional ones coming from the need to take a truncation (the drift in (
1.10
) is unbounded in the weight variable)
5
5

5

The reason for taking this truncation, which a priori seems a bit excessive, considering that the drift is Lipshitz, is discussed in Note
2.8
and, more importantly, the ‘regularization limit’, i.e. the limit coming from the fact that we will approximate Browninan paths with a sequence of piecewise smooth paths. Precise statements of results and the organization of the paper are given in Section
2
.

Note 1.2
.

We make some comments to compare Theorem
1.1
to the works
[
39
,
15
]
.

\bullet
In
[
39
]
the authors consider nonlinear SPDEs with multiplicative noise, and their representations in terms of weighted empirical measures of the form

(1.22)

\mathcal{V}_{t}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}A^{i}_{t}\delta_{X^{i}_{t}}\,.

To be more precise, while our particle-weight system (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) contains
N
particles (and
N
weights) and the equations for
(X^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t},A^{i,N,\varepsilon}_{t})
depend on
v^{N,\varepsilon}
(the relevant superscript here being
N
, not
\varepsilon
), the particle-weight systems considered in
[
39
]
are infinite systems,
\{X^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and the evolution of
(X^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})
depends on the measure (
1.22
). For this reason, the system
\{X^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
considered in
[
39
]

is a
particle representation
of the limiting SPDE, rather than a
particle approximation
. Differently put, in
[
39
]
the measure (
1.22
) is assumed to converge, here we prove such a convergence.

\bullet
Neither in
[
39
]
nor in
[
15
]
the equation for the weights is “divided by" the empirical measure
\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}
of the particles. This is new to this work and it is what allows us to obtain additive noise. The reweighting of the particles according to (
1.11
) could be intuitively interpreted as a form of importance sampling.

\bullet
In
[
15
]
the authors
study particle representations of SPDEs with boundary conditions. In that work the particles
\{X^{i}_{t}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
evolve independently of each other and of the weights, and start their evolution in
stationarity so that, denoting by
\pi
the stationary measure,
X^{i}_{t}
is distributed according to

\pi
for every time
t>0
and for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The SPDE at hand in
[
15
]
is well-posed in the
space
L^{2}_{\pi}
(the space
L^{2}
weighted by the measure
\pi
, see
[
15
]
for
details) and the main statement, i.e. the fact that the measure (
1.22
) is a representation
of the solution of the SPDE at hand, is then proved in the same space. This means that the noise
appearing in the SPDE is not multiplied by the solution
v
of the SPDE itself, but it is ‘multiplied
by
\pi
’ (as the weak formulation is in
L^{2}_{\pi}
). With the approach taken in

[
15
]
(where, again, the equation for the weights is not ‘divided by
\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}
’), if
the particles were not stationary and independent of each other, the noise term in the limiting SPDE
would be multiplied by the limit
\zeta
(if it exists) of the empirical measure
\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}

(
1.15
) associated to the particles - this can be seen with a reasoning similar to the one done to obtain (
1.21
). If one is
interested in obtaining additive noise, this is not much of a problem as long as the particles evolve
independently of the weights. However, in our case we need the particles to depend on the weights (in
order to obtain the correct nonlinear term in the limiting SPDE). Hence, if we retained the dependence on
\zeta
in the noise term, we would be obtaining
an SPDE with multiplicative noise (as
\zeta
would depend on
v
). For this reason we ‘divide by
\zeta^{N,\varepsilon}
’ the equation for the weights, as exemplified by the formal calculation (
1.19
).

∎

2.
Main results and strategy of proof

The main results of this paper are Theorem
2.3
, Theorem
2.4
and Theorem
2.6
below, providing particle approximations for the PDE (
1.3
), and for the SPDEs (
1.4
) and (
1.5
), respectively.

In this section we first comment on notion of solutions we will use for equations (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) and on the well-posedness of such equations. We then state Theorem
2.3
, Theorem
2.4
and Theorem
2.6
. Since the proof of Theorem
2.3
is the core of this paper - and indeed, once Theorem
2.3
is proved, the other two follow easily (we explain why later in this section) - in Section
2.1
we present and motivate the strategy of proof of Theorem
2.3
, and make several comments on the main results of this paper and on their proof.

We make the following standing assumption, which will hold throughout, and we don’t repeat it in every statement.

Assumption 2.1

(Standing assumption)
.

The coefficients
V,F,q
appearing in equations (
1.3
)-(
1.5
) are smooth functions,
V,F,q\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.

We will use both the weak and the mild formulation of equations (
1.3
)-(
1.5
). More precisely,

\rho=\rho_{t}(x):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
mild solution
(we should say ‘continuous-time
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-valued mild solution’ but we say mild solution in short) to (
1.3
) if for each
t\geq 0
,
\rho_{t}
belongs to the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
, the map
t\mapsto\rho_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
is continuous and the following equality is satisfied (as an equality in
L^{2}
) for every
t\in[0,T]
:

(2.1)

\rho_{t}(x)=e^{t\partial_{xx}}\rho_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{s})(x)\rho_{s}(x)]\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}q(x)\,dY_{s}\,,

where
\{e^{t\partial_{xx}}\}_{t>0}
denotes the heat semi-group on
\mathbb{T}
(the definition of which is recalled in (
6.2
)). The integral in the last addend of the above is to be intended as a Young integral (we give a recap on Young integration in Appendix
C
).

Similarly, a
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
-adapted, time-continuous
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-valued stochastic process is a mild solution to (
1.5
) if the equality

v_{t}(x)=e^{t\partial_{xx}}v_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*v_{s})(x)v_{s}(x)]\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}q(x)\,dW_{s}(x)\,,\,

holds in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
,
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely, for every
t\in[0,T]
, where
\left(\Omega,\hat{\mathcal{F}},\{\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\hat{\mathbb{P}}\right)
is the underlying filtered probability space, with
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
the filtration generated by the driving noise
W
in (
1.5
) (i.e. by the Brownian motions
\{w_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
appearing in (
1.2
)). In this instance, the integral appearing in the last addend is intended as a stochastic integral in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
(see
[
47
, Chapter 2]
for an introduction to stochastic integration in infinite dimensions).
The notion of mild solution for (
1.4
) is analogous so we don’t repeat it.

For any given
\rho_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the mild solution of equations (
1.3
)-(
1.5
) exists and it is unique, see Lemma
A.1
. This mild solution coincides with a weak solution (see again Lemma
A.1
). We will exploit this equivalence (which is useful as a result per se’ and in this paper it helps shortening some proofs), so we briefly recall that a
weak solution
to (
1.3
) is a function
\rho:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\rho_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
for every
t\geq 0
, the map
t\to\rho_{t}\in L^{2}
is continuous and, moreover, for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the following equality holds for every
t\geq 0

(2.2)

\langle f,\rho_{t}\rangle=\langle f,\rho_{0}\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\partial_{xx}f,\rho_{s}\rangle\,ds-\int_{0}^{t}\langle\partial_{x}f,(V+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{s})\rho_{s}\rangle\,ds+\langle f,q\rangle Y_{t},

where here
\langle\,,\,\rangle
denotes the scalar product in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.
6
6

6

With abuse of notation
\langle\,,\,\rangle
is used both for duality pairing and for
L^{2}
scalar product; it should be clear from context which is the case, but we will clarify in places.

Analogous definitions of weak solution can be stated for (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) so we won’t repeat them.

A
weak measure-valued
solution to (
1.3
) is a family
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of measures satisfying (
2.2
), where this time in (
2.2
) the brackets
\langle\,,\,\rangle
are to be intended as duality pairing, and such that
\rho_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
, where
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
is the space of finite signed measures on the torus endowed with the total variation norm.

We emphasize that the solutions we deal with are weak in PDE sense, but will always be strong solutions in probabilistic sense.

In what follows we will also consider three additional probability spaces
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P}\right)
,
\left(\Omega,\bar{\mathcal{F}},\{\bar{\mathcal{F}_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0},\bar{\mathbb{P}}\right)
and
\left({\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\{\tilde{\mathcal{F}_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0},\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\right)
where
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the filtration generated by the Brownian motions
\{\beta_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{\mathbb{N}}}
driving the equation of the particles (see e.g. (
2.3
) below),
\{\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the filtration generated by the single Brownian motion
\{w_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
driving SPDE (
1.4
) while
\{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the filtration generated by
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
,
\{\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and by
\{\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.
Corresponding expectations will be denoted by
\mathbb{E},\bar{\mathbb{E}},\tilde{\mathbb{E}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}
, respectively.

Let us now come to present the main results of this paper, starting from Theorem
2.3
, which contains a particle approximation result for the PDE (
1.3
).
As we have mentioned in the introduction, the proof of this result
is obtained by considering a sequence of smooth paths which converge to the forcing
Y_{t}
in (
1.3
). So let
\{Y^{\kappa}_{\cdot}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of paths which are piecewise
C^{1}
and such that
Y^{\kappa}_{\cdot}\xrightarrow{\kappa\to\infty}Y_{\cdot}
in
C^{\gamma}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
for some
\gamma<\alpha
.
7
7

7

We endow
C^{\alpha}
with the metric
|f|_{C^{\alpha}}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|f_{t}|+\sup\limits_{t,s\in[0,T],t\neq s}\frac{|f_{t}-f_{s}|}{|t-s|^{\alpha}}
.Without loss of generality we can assume that such an approximating family exists. Indeed, we recall that the space of piece-wise
C^{1}
functions is not dense in
C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. However, if
Y\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
then for any given
\gamma<\alpha
there exists an approximating family of
C^{1}
piece-wise functions converging to
Y
in
C^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
(see e.g.
[
50
, Theorem 2.52 and Theorem 8.27]
).
Hence, by ‘reducing’ the Hölder exponent of the forcing term we can always assume the existence of such an approximating sequence.

Let us consider the following system

(2.3)

\begin{cases}&\!\!\!\!\!\!dX_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=-\!\!\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})+\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\chi_{M}(A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})F^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{j,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\right)\!dt\!+\!\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}^{i}\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!dA_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\frac{q(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})}dY_{t}^{\kappa},\\
&\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!X^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=X_{0}^{i},\,\,\,A^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=A_{0}^{i},\end{cases}

where in the above
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and

•

\{\chi_{M}\}_{M\in\mathbb{N}}
is a family of cutoff functions,
\chi_{M}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, defined as

(2.4)

\chi_{M}(a)=\begin{cases}&a,\,\,\,|a|\leq M\\
\,\,&0,\,\,\,\,|a|\geq 2M.\end{cases}

Note that for each
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
\chi_{M}
is Lipschitz continuous, with Lipschitz constant 1 (independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
).

•

\{\Phi_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
is a family of mollifiers, i.e. it is a family of functions
\Phi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with the following properties:
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq c_{\varepsilon}>0
for every
x\in\mathbb{T}
;
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx=1
for every
\varepsilon>0
and
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly, as
\varepsilon
tends to zero, to the Dirac delta at zero,
\delta_{0}
. To fix ideas, we will consider Von-Mises distributions on the torus, with mean zero and concentration parameter
\varepsilon^{-1}
, i.e.

(2.5)

\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,:=\,\frac{1}{2\pi I_{0}(\varepsilon^{-1})}e^{\varepsilon^{-1}\,\cos x},\,\qquad x\in\mathbb{T},

where
I_{0}
denotes the modified Bessel function of the first kind of order 0 (see also
[
1
]
for further details),

I_{0}(\varepsilon^{-1})\,=\,\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\varepsilon)^{-2k}}{(k!)^{2}},\,\,\,\varepsilon>0.

•

\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the empirical measure associated to the particles
X^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, that is

(2.6)

\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dx):=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}(dx),

so that
\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is a random probability measure, i.e.
\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
for each
N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,t
fixed, where
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
denotes the space of probability measures on the torus
\mathbb{T}
.

Let us now introduce the following weighted empirical distribution :

(2.7)

\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dx):=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}A^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t}\cdot\delta_{X^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t}}(dx)\,.

For each
N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,t
fixed,
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is a random signed measure, i.e.
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
. In what follows, we make the following assumptions on the initial data.

Assumption 2.2
.

(1)

The initial data
(X_{0}^{i},A_{0}^{i})
of the particles system (
2.3
) are
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable i.i.d random variables with distribution
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
where
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
is the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
with finite second moment .

(2)

The joint distribution
\mu_{0}
has a smooth density, which, with an abuse of notation, we keep denoting by
\mu_{0}
.
8
8

8

Here and throughout, with abuse of notation, we use the same letter to denote a measure and its density, when it exists.

(3)

The
\mathbb{T}
-marginal
\zeta_{0}
of
\mu_{0}
, namely
\zeta_{0}(x):=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{0}(x,a)da
, is strictly positive i.e.
\eta:=\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\zeta_{0}(x)>0.

(4)

The function
\rho_{0}(x):=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}a\mu_{0}(x,a)da
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.

Note that
4
is not an extra assumption as it follows from
1
and
8
, but we spell it out for later ease of exposition.
We are interested in proving that
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
in (
2.7
) converges to
\rho_{t}
, solution to (
1.3
), in weak sense; this is the content of Theorem
2.3
below.

Theorem 2.3
.

Under Assumption
2.2
the weighted empirical measure
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
(defined in (
2.7
)) converges weakly to
\rho_{t}
solution of (
1.3
).
9
9

9

This result still holds if we had replaced
1
in Assumption
2.2
with the condition that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
i=1,\cdots,N
the pairs
(X_{0}^{i},A_{0}^{i})
have distribution
\mu_{0}^{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
such that the empirical distribution
\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{0}^{i}\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}\mu_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
.
Namely, for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the following holds:

\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}\rangle\right|=0,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

where
\rho_{t}
is the weak solution of the PDE (
1.3
) with initial datum
\rho_{0}
defined by
4
in Assumption
2.2
.

The proof of Theorem
2.3
is in four steps, corresponding to taking the four limits in the statement of the theorem. We explain the strategy of proof in Subsection
2.1
below. More explicitly, the proof of Theorem
2.3
is a consequence of Proposition
2.7
(limit
N\rightarrow\infty
), Proposition
2.9
(limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
), Proposition
2.11
(limit
M\rightarrow\infty
) and Proposition
2.12
(limit
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
), which are stated in Subsection
2.1
and proved in Section
4
to Section
7
, respectively.

Since solutions to (
1.4
) are built in probabilistically strong sense, Theorem
2.4
below is just a straightforward consequence of Theorem
2.3
. Indeed, solutions to (
1.4
) are built by simply fixing a realization
w_{t}
of the driving Brownian motion; since the path of such a realization is (
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely)
\alpha
- Hölder (for any
\alpha<1/2
), once the driving path is fixed, equation (
1.4
) is just a specific instance of equation (
1.3
).
With this in mind, let
\bar{\omega}\in\left(\Omega,\bar{\mathcal{F}},\{\bar{\mathcal{F}_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0},\bar{\mathbb{P}}\right)
(by using this shorthand notation we mean that
\bar{\omega}
belongs to
\Omega
, with
\Omega
endowed with the
\sigma
-algebra
\bar{\mathcal{F}}
and the probability measure
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
) be such that
w_{t}{(\bar{\omega})}
is a Hölder continuous realization of the noise driving (
1.4
) and then let
u_{t}(\bar{\omega})
be the corresponding solution.
Let
\bar{Y}^{\kappa}_{\cdot}
be a sequence of paths converging to
w_{t}(\bar{\omega})
in
C^{\gamma}
, as
\kappa\to\infty
, for some
\gamma<1/2
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Under Assumption
2.2
and with the notation introduced so far, let
\bar{\rho_{t}}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
be the weighted empirical measure constructed as in (
2.7
) and associated to the particle-weight system (
2.3
) where the family of trajectories

\{{Y}^{\kappa}_{\cdot}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
is replaced by the family

\{\bar{Y}^{\kappa}_{\cdot}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
described above. Then the following limit holds:

(2.8)

\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,u_{t}(\bar{\omega})\rangle\right|=0,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

where
u_{t}
is the weak solution to the SPDE (
1.4
) with initial datum
\rho_{0}
defined in
4
of Assumption
2.2
.

Proof.

with the comments made so far, the proof of Theorem
2.4
is a straightforward application of Theorem
2.3
.
∎

We emphasize that the limit (
2.8
) holds
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely. That is, Theorem
2.4
is to be read as follows: we first fix a realization of the noise
w_{t}
; once this realization is fixed, there is still noise left in the system (more precisely, in
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
), namely the noise associated to the Brownian motions
\beta_{t}^{i}
in (
2.3
) and therefore the limit (
2.8
) holds almost surely with respect to this source of stochasticity.

We explain in Note
2.8
why, from a technical point of view, it is convenient to study the deterministic evolution (
1.3
) and associated system (
2.3
) in order to produce results on the stochastic evolution (
1.4
) (and (
1.5
)) and in Note
2.13
we make comments on the order in which the limits are taken.

In equation (
1.3
) the forcing term
q(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}
is the product of a smooth function of
x
, the function
q
, and of a
\alpha
-Hölder continuous path; it will be clear from the proof of Theorem
2.3
that the procedure used to construct the particle system converging to (
1.3
) can be extended without any additional effort to the case in which (
1.3
) is forced by a finite sum of such terms, see equation (
2.11
) below. That is, let
\{Y_{t}^{z}\}_{z=-m}^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, be a family of
2m+1
time-dependent paths, which are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous and consider the equation obtained by replacing the term
q(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}
in (
1.3
) with the finite sum
\sum\limits_{|z|\leq m}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{z}
(where we recall the
e_{z}
’s are the basis of
L^{2}
in (
6.3
)) - namely, equation (
2.11
) below. Fix
\gamma<\alpha
and, for every
z=-m,\cdots,m
, let
\{Y_{t}^{z,\kappa}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
be a piece-wise
C^{1}
approximation of
Y_{t}^{z}
; that is,
Y_{\cdot}^{z,\kappa}\xrightarrow{\kappa\to\infty}Y_{\cdot}^{z}
in
C^{\gamma}
. With this premise, consider the following system of interacting particles

(2.9)

\begin{cases}&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\resizebox{22609920}{}{
$dX_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}=-\!\left(\!V^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m})\!+\!\frac{1}{N}\!\!\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\chi_{M}(A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m})F^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}-X_{t}^{j,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m})\!\!\right)\!dt\!+\!\sqrt{2}d\beta_{t}^{i}$
}\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!dA_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}=\sum\limits_{|z|\leq m}\lambda_{z}\frac{e_{z}(X_{t}^{i,N,M,\varepsilon,m})}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{N,M,\varepsilon,m}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m})}dY_{t}^{z,\kappa},\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!X^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}|_{t=0}=X_{0}^{i},\,\,\,\,A^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}|_{t=0}=A_{0}^{i}.\end{cases}

and the corresponding weighted empirical measure:

(2.10)

\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}(dx):=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}\delta_{X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}}(dx),\qquad t\in[0,T].

Then, with the same proof of Theorem
2.3
the following proposition holds.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let Assumption
2.2
hold; let
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}
be the weighted empirical measure defined in (
2.10
) and
\rho_{t}^{m}
be the solution to the following equation

(2.11)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{m}=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}^{m}+\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t}^{m})\rho_{t}^{m}]+\sum\limits_{|z|\leq m}\lambda_{z}e_{z}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{z},\qquad t\in(0,T]\\
&\rho^{m}|_{t=0}=\rho_{0},\end{cases}

where
\rho_{0}
is the function defined by
4
in Assumption
2.2
. Then, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
fixed and any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
, the following limit holds

\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}^{m}\rangle\right|\,=\,0,\qquad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

The proof of the above proposition can be done by following the exact same steps of the proof of Theorem
2.3
, so we don’t repeat the whole proof - the main purpose of stating Proposition
2.5
is to more clearly explain how Theorem
2.6
below is obtained. Indeed, at this point, since the trace class noise
W
is given by (
1.2
), i.e. it is just a (weighted) infinite sum of Brownian motions (i.e. an infinite sum of
\alpha
-Hölder paths), to obtain an approximation result for the SPDE (
1.5
), as a consequence of Proposition
2.5
, it substantially suffices to let the truncation parameter
m
in (
2.9
) to infinity.

More preciely, by following the same rationale that led us to formulate Theorem
2.4
, let
\{w_{t}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
be as in (
1.2
), so that the paths
w_{t}^{z}
’s drive the dynamics (
1.5
). Let

\hat{\omega}\in\left(\Omega,\hat{\mathcal{F}},\{\hat{\mathcal{F}_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0},\hat{\mathbb{P}}\right)

be such that for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}
the path
w_{t}^{z}\left(\hat{\omega}\right)
is a
\alpha
-Hölder continuous realization of
w_{t}^{z}
(for some
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
). Then let
v_{t}(\hat{\omega})
be the corresponding solution to the SPDE (
1.5
), for a given initial datum
v_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.
For every given
z\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\hat{Y}^{z,\kappa}_{\cdot}
be a sequence of piece-wise
C^{1}
paths converging, as
\kappa\to\infty
, to
w_{\cdot}^{z}(\hat{\omega})
(in
C^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma<\alpha
). Then the following holds.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let Assumption
2.2
hold. Suppose there exists
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
such that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}|z|^{4\delta}\lambda_{z}^{2}<+\infty
and let
\{\hat{\rho}_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
denote the weighted empirical measure (
2.10
) associated to system (
2.9
) with
\hat{Y}_{t}^{z,\kappa}
in place of
Y_{t}^{z,\kappa}
, for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the following limit holds:

\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\hat{\rho_{t}}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,v_{t}(\hat{\omega})\rangle\right|=0,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.,

where
v_{t}
is the solution of the SPDE (
1.5
) with initial datum
v_{0}
equal to
\rho_{0}
(where
\rho_{0}
is as in
4
of Assumption
2.2
).

The proof of Theorem
2.6
can be found in Section
8
. The assumption on the decay of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{z}
appears in the above theorem to guarantee well-posedness of (
1.5
) (and, as a consequence, it is used when taking the limit in
m
, detail in Section
8
).

2.1.
Strategy of proof of Theorem
2.3

From this moment onward, unless otherwise stated we make Assumption
2.2
a standing assumption so we don’t repeat it in every statement.

As already mentioned, the proof of Theorem
2.3
is divided into four steps, corresponding to taking the limits
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}
,
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}
,
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}
and
\lim\limits_{\kappa\to\infty}
(in this order). Let us explain how each of the steps is dealt with.

\bullet

Letting the number of particles
N
to infinity.
This is the most standard step.
We start by studying the convergence of the pair
\left(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)
solving
(
2.3
), as
N\to\infty
. More precisely, in Section
4
(see Proposition
4.3
), we prove that the empirical measure
\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
of the pair
\left(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)
, namely,

(2.12)

\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dxda):=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{(X^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t},A^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t})}(dxda)\,,

converges to the probability measure
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
which is the law of the process
\left(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)
, solution of the following McKean-Vlasov SDE :

(2.13)

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\,=\,X_{0}\,-\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(V^{\prime}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})+\Gamma_{M}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\right)\,ds\,+\,\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{t},\\
&A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\,=\,A_{0}\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\frac{q(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})}\,dY^{\kappa}_{s},\end{cases}

where in the above
\beta_{t}
is a standard Brownian motion on the torus,
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}:=\mathcal{L}\left(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)
(here and throughout
\mathcal{L}
denotes the law of a stochastic process),
\Gamma_{M}
is defined as

(2.14)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu)\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{M}(a)\cdot F^{\prime}(x-y)\,\mu(dyda),\quad\mbox{for every }\,x\in\mathbb{T},\,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})

and
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the
\mathbb{T}
-marginal of the law
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
of the pair
(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
, namely,

(2.15)

\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dx)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dxda),\qquad t\in[0,T].

In view of this result, the marginal
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
can be interpreted as the limit (in weak sense) as
N\to\infty
of the empirical measure
\{\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in (
2.6
) associated with the particles
X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M.\kappa}
(we will not use this fact, but it is still useful to point it out for general understanding).
Moreover, in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the pair
\left(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)
converges to the solution
(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
of (
2.13
), see again Proposition
4.3
.

Using e.g. the methods of
[
11
]
, one can show (see Proposition
2.14
at the end of this section) that the limit measure
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the unique weak measure-valued solution of the following PDE

(2.16)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right]-\frac{q(x)}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}\partial_{a}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\mu^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\mu_{0}.\end{cases}

Note that the above equation is a closed equation for
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
: while
\zeta^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t}
appears in the equation,
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
depends on
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
only (see (
2.15
)). We also note that, in establishing well-posedness of (
2.16
), it is clearly crucial that
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
is bounded below for each
\varepsilon
fixed, see proof of Proposition
2.14
.

Since
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
solves (
2.16
), the marginal
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
solves the following (linear) PDE

(2.17)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\right)\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right]\\
&\zeta^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0}.\end{cases}

This can be formally seen either by integrating (
2.16
) with respect to the variable
a\in\mathbb{R}
or using Itô’s formula; indeed, since

\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the marginal on
\mathbb{T}
of
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
,
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
satisfies

\langle f,\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left(f(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\right),\qquad\text{for all }f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\,.

Hence, using Itô’s formula, we have

\displaystyle df(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})=f^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})dX_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}+f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})dt

\displaystyle=-f^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\left[V^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})+\Gamma_{M}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\right]dt+\sqrt{2}f^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})d\beta_{t}+f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})dt\,

which, after taking expectation, formally gives the PDE (
2.17
). Let us also note that, since (
2.17
) is linear, from classical results in PDE theory, see e.g.
[
24
, Chapter 7]
or
[
27
]
, the solution to (
2.17
) is always a smooth function (since all the coefficients are smooth and the initial datum
\zeta_{0}
is chosen to be smooth as well). In the proofs of Section
4
we will not use explicitly the fact that
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
solves (
2.17
). However it is important to note it here as a motivation for the way we proceed in the next steps of the proof.

While the above facts are instrumental to the proof, we are not interested in the limit of
\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
but in the limit of
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, defined in (
2.7
). Since
\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
converges to
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, upon defining the measure
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
as follows

(2.18)

\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dx):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dxda),

(note that for each
t>0
,
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is a signed measure on the torus
\mathbb{T}
), we expect
\{\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
to converge to
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
. One can at least formally obtain the equation solved by
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
by multiplying equation (
2.16
) by
a\in\mathbb{R}
and integrating with respect to
a\in\mathbb{R}
. This is the intuition behind Proposition
2.7
below, the proof of which can be found in Section
4
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Fix
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon>0
and let
\{\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
be as in (
2.7
) and (
2.18
), respectively.
Then the following limit holds:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle\right|=0\\
,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore,
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is a weak measure-valued (and, a posteriori, classical solution) to the following PDE

(2.19)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right]+\frac{q(x)\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(x)}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(x)}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\rho^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\rho_{0},\end{cases}

where
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is defined in (
2.15
).

Note 2.8
.

Let us make some comments to motivate our strategy of proof.

\bullet
Introducing the cutoff
\chi_{M}
is strictly speaking not needed for the proof of the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
(but it does make the proof of this step easier, see the calculations around (
4.15
), which would be otherwise slightly more complicated). The place where we need it is in the proof of the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
: by looking at (
2.19
) it is clear that, when letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, one way to proceed is to obtain lower bounds which are uniform in
\varepsilon
for the quantity
\Phi_{\varepsilon}\ast\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
. The presence of the cutoff makes it easier to obtain such lower bounds. For more detail see Note
5.6
.

\bullet
If the piece-wise
C^{1}
forcing
\{Y_{t}^{\kappa}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
in (
2.3
) was replaced
by a standard Brownian motion
\{w_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
then in the equation for
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
an additional second order term would arise; that is, the empirical measure (
2.12
) would converge to a measure, say
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}
, the evolution of which would not be described by (
2.16
), but rather by the following SPDE

(2.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}

\displaystyle=\partial_{xx}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{q^{2}(x)}{(\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M})^{2}}\partial_{aa}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}

\displaystyle+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M})\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}\right]-\frac{q(x)}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}}\partial_{a}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}\partial_{t}w_{t}\,,

see
[
11
]
. Throughout the paper we will often need to estimate moments of the form
\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu(dx,da)
, with
\mu
being either
\mu^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
or
\mu^{M,\kappa}
(the latter defined by (
2.22
)). If the second order term (the second addend on the right hand side of the above) appears, estimating this quantity becomes more involved. This problem does not seem to be ameliorated by working directly with the McKean-Vlasov processes (
2.13
)/(
2.21
) rather than with the equation for the corresponding joint densities. Another option would be to work directly with the equation for
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
or
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, see (
2.19
) and (
2.26
), as these are additive equations (as opposed to the corresponding equations for the joint density, which are multiplicative). However, it seems to us that since
\Gamma_{M}
depends on
\mu
, we would still end up needing estimates on moments of the joint density
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
or
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
.

\bullet
It is crucial to notice that, whether we fix the noise (and its sequence of approximants
Y^{\kappa}
) and hence end up considering the joint distribution
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
in (
2.16
) or we keep the original Brownian motion and hence study the joint distribution
\mu^{\varepsilon,M}
in (
2.20
), the corresponding weighted marginal that we would obtain is the same. That is, if we set
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a\,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}(dadx)
, then the equation satisfied by
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M}
(obtained by multiplying equation (
2.20
) by
a\in\mathbb{R}
and then integrating with respect to
a\in\mathbb{R}
), is the same as equation (
2.19
) satisfied by
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
. This is also true for the equations satisfied by all the joint distributions that will appear when we consider subsequent limits (namely when we consider
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
,
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
satisfying (
2.22
), (
2.30
), respectively) and their weighted marginals (
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
,
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
satisfying (
2.26
), (
2.33
), respectively). Therefore we can interpret the idea of fixing the noise as follows: we are interested in the limiting behaviour of a measure,
\rho^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, which we can be constructed as the (weighted) marginal of many potential probability distributions; what we do by fixing the noise is to realise
\rho^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t}
and its limit
\rho_{t}
(as well as its ‘intermediate limits’
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
and
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
) as (weighted) marginals of a specific joint distribution, which we choose in a way to simplify the proof.
∎

\bullet

Limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

Let us begin with noting that since the mollifiers
\{\Phi_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
converge to the Dirac delta as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, if we assume that
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
converges to some limit
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
and hence

\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
converges to a function, say
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, then we would obtain
\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
. So, intuitively, in the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we expect the stochastic process
(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
in (
2.13
) to converge to
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
, solution to the following system:

(2.21)

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{M,\kappa}\,=\,X_{0}\,-\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(V^{\prime}(X_{s}^{M,\kappa})+\Gamma_{M}(X_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa})\right)\,ds\,+\,\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{t},\\
&A_{t}^{M,\kappa}\,=\,A_{0}\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\frac{q(X_{s}^{M,\kappa})}{\zeta_{s}^{M,\kappa}(X_{s}^{M,\kappa})}\,dY^{\kappa}_{s}\,,\end{cases}

where
\mu^{M,\kappa}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
. This is the correct ‘guess’ but, for the purposes of the proof, we need to be careful how we define
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
. Indeed, if we were to proceed as in the previous step we would now define
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
to be the
\mathbb{T}
-marginal of
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, namely,

\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dx):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda),\,\,\,\,\,t\in[0,T]\,.

We would then observe that

\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
solves the following PDE

(2.22)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right]-\frac{q(x)}{\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}}\partial_{a}\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\mu^{M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\mu_{0},\end{cases}

and hence
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
solves the Cauchy problem

(2.23)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\right)\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right]\\
&\zeta^{M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0}\,.\end{cases}

Again, the PDE satisfied by
\zeta^{M,\kappa}_{t}
can be formally deduced by integrating (
2.22
) with respect to the variable
a\in\mathbb{R}
. If we were to proceed in this way then equation (
2.22
) would have the same structure as the PDE (
2.16
); in particular, this would be a closed equation in
\mu^{M,\kappa}_{t}
(as before,
\zeta^{M,\kappa}_{t}
would depend on
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
only). However, with these definitions, we would not know how to study the well-posedness of equation (
2.22
), because now in the denominator of the last addend of (
2.22
) there is no mollifier, so we don’t know whether the denominator is strictly positive. In particular, the assumptions of
[
11
]
, which we use to prove well-posedness of (
2.16
), are not satisifed in this case. To overcome this issue, and guided by the intuition that if
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
was defined as the marginal of the joint law
