\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
then
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
would solve the PDE (
2.23
), what we will prove is that
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
converges, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, to
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, which is the law of the process
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
, where in equation (
2.21
) we define
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
as the solution of the PDE (
2.23
), see Definition
5.1
. We prove this fact in Section
5
, see Proposition
5.3
in particular. The advantage of defining
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
as solution of the PDE (
2.23
) comes from the fact that, from classical PDE theory, we easily know that the solution to (
2.23
) is positive (see Lemma
5.2
), fact which makes the study of the well-posedness of (
2.22
)-(
2.23
) (see Proposition
2.14
) easily accessible.

Analogously to what we did when establishing the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
, we now define the following measure

(2.24)

\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dx):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda),\,\,\,\,t\in[0,T],

which is a signed measure on the torus and it is the object we are actually interested in.
In Section
5
we prove that the following holds.

Proposition 2.9
.

Fix
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\{\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\}_{\varepsilon>0}\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
and
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
be the measures defined in (
2.18
) and (
2.24
), respectively. Then, for each
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, the following limit holds

(2.25)

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\,\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}\rangle\right|=0,

for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is a weak measure-valued (and, a posteriori, classical) solution to the PDE

(2.26)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right]+q(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\rho^{M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\rho_{0}\,.\end{cases}

As a consequence, the solution
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
of the PDE (
2.19
) converges (in the sense (
2.25
)) to
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, solution of the PDE (
2.26
).

Note 2.10
.

In comparing the PDE (
2.19
) solved by
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and the PDE (
2.26
) solved by
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, it should be noted that the forcing term of the former equation is not purely additive, while the one of equation (
2.26
) is. So, as expected from the heuristics of Section
1.2
, see (
1.19
), it is in taking the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
that we ’simplify’ the multiplicative term in front of the forcing term and obtain a pure additive forcing.

∎

\bullet

The limit
M\to\infty
.

In this third step we let the cut-off parameter
M
to infinity. Informally, since
\Gamma_{M}
(defined in (
2.14
)) converges, as
M\to\infty
, to the function
\Gamma
given by

(2.27)

\Gamma(x,\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}aF^{{}^{\prime}}(x-y)\mu(dyda),\,\qquad\quad x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,\,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}),

assuming that the pair
(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
converges to some limit
(\mu_{t}^{\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{\kappa})
, we would intuitively expect
\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\xrightarrow{M\to\infty}\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})
. Hence, we also expect that the stochastic process
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
, solution to (
2.21
), converges to a stochastic process,
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
, solution to the following SDE:

(2.28)

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{\kappa}\,=\,X_{0}\,-\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(V^{\prime}(X_{s}^{\kappa})+\Gamma(X_{s}^{\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\right)\,ds\,+\,\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{t}\\
&A_{t}^{\kappa}\,=\,A_{0}\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\frac{q(X_{s}^{\kappa})}{\zeta_{s}^{\kappa}(X_{s}^{\kappa})}\,dY_{s}^{\kappa}\,,\end{cases}

where
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
and, similarly to (
2.21
), the function
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
is defined as the (unique) classical solution to the (linear) PDE

(2.29)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}\right]\\
&\zeta^{\kappa}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0}.\end{cases}

This turns out to be the correct guess and we prove this fact in Proposition
6.4
.
Analogously to previous steps, it can be shown (see Proposition
2.14
) that if
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
is the law of the McKean-Vlasov SDE (
2.28
), then
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
is a measure-valued solution of the following equation:

(2.30)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\mu_{t}^{\kappa}\right]-\frac{q(x)}{\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}}\partial_{a}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\mu^{M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\mu_{0},\end{cases}

where, for clarity, we iterate that
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
is the solution to (
2.29
).
As in previous sections, let us set

(2.31)

\rho_{t}^{\kappa}(dx):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)\,

and notice that, since the drift
\Gamma_{M}
has been ‘replaced’, in the limit, by the drift
\Gamma
, we have

(2.32)

\displaystyle\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}aF^{{}^{\prime}}(x-y)\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dady)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}F^{{}^{\prime}}(x-y)\rho^{\kappa}_{t}(dy)=F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t}^{\kappa}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}\,.

Using (
2.32
), one expects
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
to be the solution of equation (
2.33
) below. In Section
6
we then prove that the following result holds.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. Let
\{\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}\}_{M\in\mathbb{N}}
be the family of measures defined in (
2.24
) and
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
be as in (
2.31
). Then the following limit holds:

\lim_{M\to\infty}\,\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\rangle\right|=0,\,

for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.
Furthermore,
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
is a weak measure-valued (and, a posteriori, classical) solution to the following PDE:

(2.33)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}^{\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t}^{\kappa})\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\right]+q(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{t=0}=\rho_{0},\end{cases}

As a consequence, the solution
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
of the PDE (
2.26
) converges, as
M\rightarrow\infty
, to
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
solution to the PDE (
2.33
).

Equation (
2.33
) is a closed equation for
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
, as opposed to the evolutions for
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, equations (
2.19
) and (
2.22
), respectively, which are not closed in their respective unknowns,
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
.

To prove Proposition
2.11
, we will use the fact (due to (
2.32
)) that the system (
2.28
)-(
2.29
) can be recast into an equivalent form, where the joint distribution
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
does not directly appear. That is, the evolution (
2.28
)-(
2.29
) is equivalent to the following

(2.34)

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{\kappa}\,=\,X_{0}\,-\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(V^{\prime}(X_{s}^{\kappa})+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{s}^{\kappa}(X_{s}^{\kappa})\right)\,ds\,+\,\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{t},\\
&A_{t}^{\kappa}\,=\,A_{0}\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\frac{q(X_{s}^{\kappa})}{\zeta_{s}^{\kappa}(X_{s}^{\kappa})}\,dY_{s}^{\kappa},\\
&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t}^{\kappa})\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}\right],\\
&\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}^{\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t}^{\kappa})\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\right]+q(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa},\\
&\zeta^{\kappa}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0},\,\,\rho^{\kappa}|_{t=0}=\rho_{0}.\end{cases}

As one can see, the perk of system (
2.34
) is that the evolution of the pair
\left(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa}\right)
is now linear, as in the above
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
is just the solution of a PDE that can be solved independently of all the other equations in the system. Of course, this ‘linearity’ comes at the price of having to increase dimensionality, as system (
2.34
) comes with the additional equation for
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
to the set of SDEs and PDEs governing the system. The equivalence between the system (
2.28
)- (
2.29
) and system (
2.34
) is rather straightforward, and explained in detail in Section
6
, see Note
6.3
.

\bullet

Limit
\kappa\to\infty
.

We are left with showing the convergence of
\{\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
to
\rho_{t}
solution to (
1.3
). This is the content of the proposition below, the proof of which is contained in Section
7
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
be the solution to the PDE (
2.33
); then
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
converges, as
\kappa\to\infty
, to
\rho_{t}
solution to (
1.3
), in the sense that the following limit holds:

\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\,\,\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}\rangle\right|=0\ ,

for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.

Note 2.13
.

Let us make further comments on our results and on the strategy of proof.

•

We expect all four limits i.e.
\lim\limits_{\kappa\to\infty}
,
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}
,
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}
and
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}
to commute; the order in which we take them is the one we found easiest, and crucial to our proof is to take the limit
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
last. If we took it sooner then the Stratonovich second order correction term in (
2.20
) would appear, presenting the difficulties we have discussed in Note
2.8
. On this point, it is important to note that while, when taking the limits in
N,\varepsilon
and
M
, the limit for
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
,
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}
was deduced from the limit for the joint distribution
\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
,
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, respectively, in the last step, when we let
\kappa
to infinity, we never study the limit for
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
, and instead we study directly the limit of
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
. This is due to two things: firstly, the equation (
2.33
) for
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
and the equation (
1.3
) for
\rho_{t}
have the same structure - in particular, they are both closed equations in the respective unknowns, so that the joint distributions
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
and
\mu_{t}
do not appear in such equations, hence there is no need to study their convergence. Moreover, if we were to study the limit for
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
, then one can see that
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
would converge to
\mu_{t}
, solution of the following equation:

(2.35)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\partial_{xx}\mu_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{q^{2}(x)}{\zeta_{t}^{2}(x)}\partial_{aa}\mu_{t}+\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}))\mu_{t}]-\frac{q(x)}{\zeta_{t}(x)}\partial_{a}\mu_{t}\partial_{t}Y

where
\zeta_{t}
solves the following PDE

\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}+\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}))\zeta_{t}]\,.

In particular, and as expected, since the equation for
\mu^{\kappa}
is multiplicative, the second order correction term does appear in (
2.35
). Studying the evolution (
2.35
) would also require the use of rough path theory.

•

Still on the order in which we take limits, as we said we take here limits in a specific order and, especially, we take them one at a time. We refer the reader to Note
5.6
to explain the detail of why we do so, but, in essence, this is because we were only able to obtain estimates which are uniform in one parameter at a time. There is a question about whether these limits can be taken together. If one was to try and do so then we believe that
\varepsilon
should be chosen to scale with
N
.

∎

We conclude this section with the statement of Proposition
2.14
, the proof of which is postponed to Appendix
A
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
(
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
,
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
, respectively) be defined as the law of
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})

\left((X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa}),\,(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa}),\,\text{respectively}\right)
solution to the system (
2.13
) ((
2.21
), (
2.28
), respectively). Then
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is a weak measure valued solution to (
2.16
) (equation (
2.22
), (
2.30
), respectively). Furthermore
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
,
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
and
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
belong to the space
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))
.
10
10

10

For clarity we recall that (
2.13
) is intended as a closed equation for
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, while (
2.22
) and (
2.30
) are not closed equations; so, when we say that
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is a weak measure-valued solution to (
2.22
) we really mean that the pair
(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
is a solution to (
2.22
)-(
2.23
), with (
2.22
) to be intended in weak measure-valued sense and (
2.23
) in classical sense. Similarly for the pair (
2.30
)-(
2.29
). Moreover we point out that while the notion of weak measure valued solution for
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
can be given as at the beginning of Section
2
i.e. in particular in the space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
, the notion of weak measure-valued solution for
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
is intended in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
.

3.
Notation and Preliminaries

In this section we set the notation and state some background results needed later in the paper. Let us consider a complete and separable metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
and let
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
be the space of probability measures on
\mathcal{X}
. Unless otherwise stated the
\sigma
-algebra provided on
\mathcal{X}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X})
. Following
[
40
, Section 2 and Theorem 3.3]
we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X})
the space of probability measures
\mu
on
\mathcal{X}
such that

\int_{\mathcal{X}}d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x_{0})\,\mu(dx)\,<\,\infty\ ,

and

(3.1)

\int_{\mathcal{X}}d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x_{0})^{2}\,\mu(dx)\,<\,\infty\ ,

respectively, for any arbitrary reference point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
.
11
11

11

A straightforward calculation shows that (
3.1
) does not depend on the choice of the point.

Given two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
(or
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X})
), we introduce the space

\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}(\mu,\nu):=\Big\{\pi\in\mathcal{P}\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\right)\big|\pi(S\times\mathcal{X})=\mu(S),\;\pi(\mathcal{X}\times S)=\nu(S)\,,\,\mathrm{for\;every\;Borel\;set\;}S\subseteq\mathcal{X}\Big\}\ ,

and define the 2-Wasserstein metric on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X})
by

(3.2)

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\,:=\,\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)^{2}\,\pi(dxdy).

Equivalently, we can write

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\,=\,\inf_{X\sim\mu,\,\overline{X}\sim\nu}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\nu}\left[d_{\mathcal{X}}(X,\overline{X})^{2}\right],

where
X
and
\overline{X}
are
\mathcal{X}
-valued random variables with given
distribution
\mu
and
\nu
, respectively, and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\nu}
denotes the expectation with respect to the joint law of
\mu
and
\nu
. An analogous definition for the 1-Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{1}
on
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
holds, see
[
49
]
.
We will often use a dual formulation of the Wasserstein distance due to Kantorovich (cfr.
[
40
, Theorem 2.15]
and
[
49
, Theorem 5.10]
), namely,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},1}(\mu,\nu)=\sup\left\{\langle f,\mu\rangle-\langle f,\nu\rangle|f,g\in\text{Lip}_{1}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})\right\}

where Lip
{}_{1}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})
denotes the class of real-valued Lipschitz continuous functions on
\mathcal{X}
with Lipschitz constant less than or equal to 1 while for
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}(\mu,\nu)=\sup\left\{\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(x)\mu(dx)+\int_{\mathcal{X}}g(x)\nu(dx)\bigg|f\in L^{1}(\mu),g\in L^{1}(\nu),f(x)+g(y)\leq d_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(x,y)\right\},

where
L^{1}(\mu)
,
L^{1}(\nu)
denote the space of functions on
\mathcal{X}
, integrable w.r.t
\mu
and
\nu
, respectively. Moreover, from Jensen’s inequality we obtain

(3.4)

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}(\mu,\nu),\qquad\text{for any $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X})$}.

We will often use the following characterization for the convergence in the 2-Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}
. In what follows
C\left(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R}\right)
denotes the space of real-valued continuous functions on
\mathcal{X}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

[
40
, Theorem 2.13]

Let
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
be a complete and separable metric space and let
\{\mu^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\,\,\mu\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X})
then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{X},2}(\mu^{n},\mu)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0

(2)

For any
f\in C(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})
for which there exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
and
c>0
such that
|f(x)|\leq c\,\,d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x_{0})^{2}
, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the following holds:

\left|\langle f,\mu^{n}\rangle-\langle f,\mu\rangle\right|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

In the next sections we mainly apply the above results to
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
endowed with the metric

d_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}((x,a),(y,b)):=\sqrt{\min\left\{|x-y|,\,2\pi-|x-y|\right\}^{2}\,+\,|a-b|^{2}},\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{T},a,b\in\mathbb{R},

and to
\mathcal{X}=C_{t}:=C\left([0,t];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\right)
the space of time-continuous
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
-valued functions defined up to a given time
t>0
endowed with the supremum metric

|(x_{\cdot},y_{\cdot})|_{t}:=\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}d_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}(x_{s},y_{s}),\qquad

having used in the above the notation
x_{\cdot}=\{x_{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
for
x_{\cdot}\in C_{t}
, and similarly for
y_{\cdot}\in C_{t}
. Unless otherwise stated, we denote the 2-Wasserstein metric on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(C_{t})
by
\mathcal{W}_{2}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2,t}
, respectively. In addition to that, the spaces
\Pi_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}
and
\Pi_{C_{t}}
will be denoted by
\Pi_{2}
and
\Pi_{t}
, respectively.
We also recall the following inequality between
\mathcal{W}_{2}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2,t}
, which we will use multiple times:

(3.5)

\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu_{s})\leq\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot},\nu_{\cdot}),

where in the above we have used the notation
\mu_{\cdot}=\{\mu_{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
and
\nu_{\cdot}=\{\nu_{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
for
\mu_{\cdot},\nu_{\cdot}\in C([0,t];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))
with
t\geq 0
.
When using the dual pairing between measures and functions we will need to distinguish between the case when the measure is on
\mathbb{T}
and when it is on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
; when
\rho\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
we denote dual pairing by
\langle f,\rho\rangle
, when
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
and
\Psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
we denoted it by
\langle\langle\Psi,\mu\rangle\rangle
.

As customary, we let

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}):=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\,\,\,:\,\,\,\partial_{x}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right\}

where the spatial derivative
\partial_{x}f
is taken in the weak sense and, for every
n>1
,

(3.6)

H^{n}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}):=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\,\,\,:\,\,\,\partial_{x}f\in H^{n-1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right\}.

We endow such spaces with the following norms:

\displaystyle|f|_{H^{n}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}:=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}|\partial_{x}^{i}f|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2},\,\,\,\,f\in H^{n}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}),\,\,\,\,n\in\mathbb{N},

where
\partial_{x}^{i}f
denotes the
i
-th weak derivative of
f
.
In the proofs we use frequently the notation
x\lesssim y
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, which means
x\leq Cy
for some constant
C\geq 0
, the dependence of which on certain parameters is specified if necessary. For instance, by
x\lesssim_{t}y
we mean that the constant
C
depends on
t
.

Lastly, let us point out the following fact. In what comes next two types of integrals appear. The first one, with respect to piece-wise
C^{1}
-functions
Y^{\kappa}
and the second one with respect to Hölder continuous paths, namely

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}f_{s}\,dY_{s}^{\kappa},\,f\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R})\,\,\text{and}\,\,\int_{0}^{t}g_{s}\,dY_{s},\,\,g\in C^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R}).

The first type of integral is intended in the Riemann-Stieltjes sense, while the second type of integral is intended in the Young sense. We refer the reader to Appendix
C
for a brief recap on Young integral, see
[
42
, Sec 1.1]
for a comprehensive introduction.

4.
The limit
N\to\infty

The main purpose of this section is to prove Proposition
2.7
. The proof of Proposition
2.7
will be a straightforward consequence of Proposition
4.3
below. Hence, we focus our attention on proving the latter proposition, which is concerned with studying convergence, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, of the empirical measure

\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
defined in (
2.12
) to
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, law of (
2.13
).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(X_{0},A_{0}):\Omega\to\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable with distribution
\mu_{0}
(which we recall satisfies the standing Assumption
2.2
).
We say that the triple
t\to(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\in C([0,T];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))
is a solution to (
2.13
) if

(i)

t\to(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is an
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
-adapted stochastic process;

(ii)

The stochastic process
(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
solves (
2.13
), for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. where
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\mathcal{L}\left(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)
,
\Gamma_{M}
has been defined in (
2.14
) and
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the
\mathbb{T}
-marginal of
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, as defined in (
2.15
).

From Proposition
2.14
we know that
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the unique measure-valued solution to the PDE (
2.16
).
To streamline calculations we introduce the following operator

(4.1)

\displaystyle\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\mu)\,:=\,\frac{q(x)}{\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mu(dyda)},\qquad x\in\mathbb{T},\,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}).

With this notation, the dynamics of the weights
A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
in (
2.3
) and of the process
A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
in (
2.13
) can be rewritten as

(4.2)

\displaystyle dA_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\Xi_{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})dY_{t}^{\kappa},\,\,\,i=1,\cdots,N

and

(4.3)

\displaystyle dA_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\Xi_{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})dY_{t}^{\kappa},

respectively. Indeed, from definition (
2.6
) of
\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and since the mollifier
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
depends only the spatial variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\zeta_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dy)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dyda),

which gives (
4.2
). Equation (
4.3
) can be obtained similarly. Using (
4.3
), the evolution (
2.13
) can be rewritten as follows

(4.4)

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\left[V^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}),+\Gamma_{M}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\right]\,ds+\sqrt{2}\beta_{t},\\
&A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=A_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Xi_{\varepsilon}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})dY_{s}^{\kappa},\\
\end{cases}

where
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
.

Lemma 4.2
.

System (
4.4
) (or equivalently (
2.13
)) admits a unique solution in the sense of Definition
4.1
. Moreover, the pair
(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
belongs to the space
L^{2}\left(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})\right)
.

We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for the proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Proposition 4.3

(Particle approximation)
.

Let
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon,T>0
be fixed. Let
(X_{0},A_{0})
and
\mu_{0}
as in Definition
4.1
. Then the empirical distribution
\mu^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t}
of the particle system (
2.3
) (defined in (
2.12
)) converges to
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
(law of the pair
(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
solution to the McKean-Vlasov SDE (
2.13
) ) in the Wasserstein
\mathcal{W}_{2,T}
distance, i.e.

(4.5)

\lim\limits_{N\to+\infty}\mathcal{W}_{2,T}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})=0,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

As a consequence,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\left|X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right|^{2}+\left|A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}-A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right|^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,\quad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

We first prove Proposition
2.7
and then come back to the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.

From (
4.5
) and Proposition
3.1
(the latter with
\mathcal{X}=C_{T}
) we know that

(4.6)

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle-\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle\right|=0,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

for any given
\Psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
which grows at most quadratically with respect to the variable
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, from the definition of
\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\Psi(X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}),

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Psi(x,a)\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dxda)=\mathbb{E}(\Psi(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})).

In particular, if we set
\Psi(x,a)=af(x)
,
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
, from the above, (
4.6
), (
2.7
) and (
2.18
) we obtain

\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle-\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M.\kappa}\rangle|=0,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. This proves the first claim in Proposition
2.7
.
For the second claim, by recalling that
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is the weak solution to (
2.16
) and that
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is given by (
2.18
) we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\left\langle\left\langle af,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\left\langle\left\langle a\partial_{xx}f,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\left\langle\left\langle a\partial_{x}f,\left[\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}\left(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)\right)\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right]\right\rangle\right\rangle+\left\langle\left\langle\partial_{a}(af),\frac{q}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{xx}f,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{x}f,\left[\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}\left(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right)\right)\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\left\langle\left\langle f,\frac{q}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\,.

Now, since
f
depends on the variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
only, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle f,\frac{q}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}=\left\langle f,\frac{q\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}}\right\rangle\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa},

which shows that
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
is a weak measure-valued solution to (
2.19
). The fact that such a solution admits a density and that such a density is a weak, and then classical, solution to (
2.19
) is a consequence of classical PDE theory, see
[
24
, Chapter 7]
or also
[
36
]
, as equation (
2.19
) is a linear parabolic PDE with a forcing which is
C^{\infty}
in space and piece-wise
C^{1}
in time.
∎

We now state a preliminary lemma, Lemma
4.4
, which will be used to prove Proposition
4.3
. The proof of Lemma
4.4
is postponed to the end of this section.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\Gamma_{M}
and
\Xi_{\varepsilon}
be as in (
2.14
) and (
4.1
), respectively; then the following two inequalities hold for any given
x,y\in\mathbb{T}
,
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
:

(4.7)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu)-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(y,\nu)\right|^{2}\,\leq K(n,M)(\,|x-y|^{2}\,+\,\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu,\,\nu))\,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\mu)-\Xi_{\varepsilon}(y,\nu)\right|^{2}\,\leq\tilde{K}_{\varepsilon}(\,|x-y|^{2}\,+\,\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu,\,\nu)),

where the constants
K(n,M)
and
\tilde{K}_{\varepsilon}
are given by

\displaystyle K(n,M)

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{M,1\right\}|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{|q|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}D_{\varepsilon}+|q^{\prime}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}M_{\varepsilon}}{m_{\varepsilon}^{2}},

respectively, with
m_{\varepsilon}
,
D_{\varepsilon}
and
M_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
4.16
), (
4.17
) and (
4.19
), respectively.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

In what follows, to avoid notation overload and since we are considering the limit
N\to\infty
only, we omit the dependence on
M,\varepsilon,\kappa>0
for the quantities used in the proof. So, in this proof only,
X^{i,N}_{t}
and
A^{i,N}_{t}
are short notations for
X_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
and
A_{t}^{i,N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, respectively; similarly for
\mu_{t}^{N}
, which will be short for
\mu_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
. Moreover, we do not track explicitly the dependence of the constants on
M,\varepsilon,\kappa
as this is not relevant to this proof. We denote by
C(t)
a generic constant, depending continuously on
t
(and increasing w.r.t time
t
), which may change from line to line. We also point out that unless otherwise stated, the inequalities (and equalities) of this proof will hold
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. With this clarification, we start by considering
N
independent copies of the McKean-Vlasov system (
4.4
). Namely, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, let
(X_{t}^{i},A_{t}^{i})
be the solution of the following problem

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&dX^{i}_{t}\,=\,-\left(V^{\prime}(X^{i}_{t})+\Gamma_{M}(X^{i}_{t},\,\mu_{t}^{i})\right)\,dt\,+\,\sqrt{2}\,d\beta^{i}_{t},\\
&dA^{i}_{t}\,=\,\Xi_{\varepsilon}(X^{i}_{t},\,\mu_{t})\,dY_{t}^{\kappa}\ ,\\
&\mu_{t}^{i}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{i},A_{t}^{i}),\\
&X^{i}|_{t=0}=X_{0}^{i},\,\,A^{i}|_{t=0}=A_{0}^{i}.\end{cases}

For any given
i=1,\cdots,N
, the initial condition
(X_{0}^{i},A_{0}^{i})
and the Wiener process
\beta_{t}^{i}
coincide, respectively, with the initial datum and the Wiener process, respectively, appearing in the dynamics (
2.3
). Hence, as a consequence of Lemma
4.2
we obtain that
the pairs
(X_{t}^{i},A_{t}^{i})
are i.i.d. stochastic processes with same law
\mu_{t}=\mu_{t}^{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,N
.

Let us now begin with estimating the difference
\left|X^{i,N}_{s}-X^{i}_{s}\right|^{2}
.
By recalling that
X_{t}^{i,N}
solves the first SDE in (
2.3
), from Jensen’s inequality and (
4.7
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X^{i,N}_{s}-X^{i}_{s}\right|^{2}\,

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\left[\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}|X^{i,N}_{r}-X^{i}_{r}|^{2}\,dr\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\Gamma_{M}(X^{i,N}_{r},\mu^{N}_{r})-\Gamma_{M}(X^{i}_{r},\mu_{r})\right|^{2}\,ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}|X^{i,N}_{r}-X^{i}_{r}|^{2}\,dr\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{N},\mu_{r})\,ds\right].

By now applying Gronwall’s inequality we get

(4.10)

\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X^{i,N}_{s}-X^{i}_{s}\right|^{2}\,\leq C(t)\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{N},\mu_{r})\,ds.

Similarly, by recalling that
A_{t}^{i,N}
solves the second SDE in (
2.3
), using (
4.2
), (
4.8
) and Jensen’s inequality we get

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|A^{i,N}_{s}-A^{i}_{s}\right|^{2}\,

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(X^{i,N}_{r},\mu^{N}_{r})-\Xi._{\varepsilon}(X^{i}_{r},\mu_{r})\right|^{2}\,|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|^{2}\,ds,

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}|X^{i,N}_{r}-X^{i}_{r}|^{2}\,dr\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{N},\mu_{r})\,ds\right].

Hence, from the above and (
4.10
), we have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left[|X^{i,N}_{s}-X^{i}_{s}|^{2}+|A^{i,N}_{s}-A^{i}_{s}|^{2}\right]\,\leq C(t)\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu^{N}_{r},\mu_{r})\,ds.

Let us now introduce the empirical measure associated to the i.i.d. particles in (
4.9
), namely,

\nu^{N}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{(X^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})}\ ,\qquad N\in\mathbb{N},\,\,t\in[0,T]\,,

and recall that, from the definitions of Section
3
(specifically from the definition of 2-Wasserstein distance (
3.2
) with
\mathcal{X}=C_{t}
), we have

(4.12)

\mathcal{W}_{2,t}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\nu_{\cdot}^{N})=\inf\limits_{\pi\in\Pi_{t}\left(\mu^{N},\nu^{N}\right)}\,\,\,\int_{C_{t}\times C_{t}}|(x,a)-(y,b)|_{t}^{2}\,\,\pi(dxdadydb)\,,

where in the above we have used the notation
\mu_{\cdot}^{N}:=\{\mu^{N}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
, and similarly for
\nu_{\cdot}^{N}
.
Hence, from (
4.11
) we have

(4.13)

\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\nu_{\cdot}^{N})\,\leq\,\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X^{i,N}_{s}-X^{i}_{s}|^{2}+|A^{i,N}_{s}-A^{i}_{s}|^{2}\right).

The above inequality can be seen to hold true by using (
4.12
) and considering the empirical measure
\pi
on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(C_{t})
defined as

\pi:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\left((X^{i,N}_{s},A^{i,N}_{s})_{s\in[0,t]},(Y^{i}_{s},A^{i}_{s})_{s\in[0,t]}\right)}\,\in\,\Pi_{t}(\mu^{N},\nu^{N}).

From (
4.11
) and (
4.13
) it then follows

(4.14)

\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\nu_{\cdot}^{N})\leq C(t)\int_{0}^{t}\,\,\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{N},\mu_{r}).

Moreover, by triangular inequality and (
4.14
) we obtain

\displaystyle\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\nu_{\cdot}^{N})+\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\nu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\,\,\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{N},\mu_{r})\,ds+\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\nu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})\right]

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{W2 leq W2t}}C(t)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,s}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})\,ds+\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\nu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})\right].

Hence, by Gronwall’s inequality we obtain

\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})\leq C(t)\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\nu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot}),\qquad t\in[0,T].

Finally, since the particles-weights
(X^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})
, are i.i.d. random variables with same distribution
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})
, from the definition of empirical distribution
\nu^{N}
and the strong law of large numbers (
[
40
, Corollary 2.14]
) for any given
t\in[0,T]
we get

\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\nu_{\cdot}^{N},\mu_{\cdot})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Let us begin with proving (
4.7
). We first note that we can move the derivative inside the integral, so that

\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{M}(a)F^{(n+1)}(x-y)\mu(dyda),\qquad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}).

From the triangle inequality and the Lipschitz continuity of
\chi_{M}
and
F^{(n+1)}
(we recall that
\chi_{M}
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant 1) we obtain, for
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu)-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(y,\nu)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu)-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu)\right|+\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu)-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(y,\nu)\right|

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{kantorovich}}|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle+|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|x-y|\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{M}(a)\,\mu(dzda)

(4.15)

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{chiM}}|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)+|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}M|x-y|

\displaystyle\leq\max\{M,1\}|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}(|x-y|+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)).

As for inequality (
4.8
) we proceed similarly. Indeed,

\displaystyle\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\mu)-\Xi_{\varepsilon}(y,\nu)\right|\leq\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\mu)-\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\nu)\right|+\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\nu)-\Xi_{\varepsilon}(y,\nu)\right|

For the first addend, by first noting from (
2.5
) that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq\frac{1}{2\pi I_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}=:m_{\varepsilon},\qquad\varepsilon>0

(4.17)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d}{dx}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}}{2\pi\varepsilon I_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}=:D_{\varepsilon},\qquad\varepsilon>0

we have

\displaystyle\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\mu)-\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\nu)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{q(x)\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mu(dyda)-\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\nu(dyda)\right)}{\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mu(dyda)\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\nu(dyda)}\right|

(4.18)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|q|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}D_{\varepsilon}}{m_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)\,.

As for the second addend, from

(4.19)

\left|\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}}{2\pi I_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}=:M_{\varepsilon},\qquad\varepsilon>0,

the lower bound (
4.16
) and the upper bound (
4.17
) we have

\displaystyle\left|\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\nu)-\Xi_{\varepsilon}(y,\nu)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{q(x)\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(y-z)\mu(dzda)-q(y)\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-z)\mu(dzda)}{\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x-z)\mu(dzda)\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(y-z)\mu(dzda)}\right|

(4.20)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|q|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}D_{\varepsilon}+|q^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}M_{\varepsilon}}{m_{\varepsilon}^{2}}|x-y|.

Combining (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) we obtain (
4.8
), which concludes the proof.
∎

5.
The limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0

In this section we prove Proposition
2.9
. This section is organised similarly to the previous one, so Proposition
2.9
will be proved as a consequence of Proposition
5.3
below. So, after some preliminary definitions, we first state Proposition
5.3
then prove Proposition
2.9
, and finally prove Proposition
5.3
itself.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})
be as in Definition
4.1
and Assumption
2.2
. A strong solution to (
2.21
) is a 4-tuple

t\to(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})\in C\left([0,T];\mathbb{T}\!\times\!\mathbb{R}\!\times\!\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})\!\times\!C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)

satisfying the conditions given below

(i)

(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
is
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
-adapted;

(ii)

The stochastic process
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
solves (
2.21
), for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s, where in (
2.21
)
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
and
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is the solution to the PDE (
2.23
).

We recall (see Proposition
2.14
) that
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is the (unique) weak measure-valued solution to the PDE (
2.22
).

Lemma 5.2
.

System (
2.21
) admits a unique solution in the sense of Definition
5.1
. Furthermore, for any given
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
the function
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is strictly positive, i.e.
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(x)>0
for every
x\in\mathbb{T}
,
t\in[0,T]
and the pair
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
belongs to the space
L^{2}\left(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})\right)
.

We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for the proof of the above lemma. Let us emphasize that the above well-posedness result only gives us the strict positivity of the function
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
for each
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
fixed (such a positivity comes as a biproduct of the scheme of proof), but the lower bound obtained through the proof of Lemma
5.2
is not uniform in
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Bounds which are uniform in
M\in\mathbb{N}
will be obtained in Lemma
6.7
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed and let
X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
be as in Definition
4.1
and Assumption
2.2
.
Let
\left(X^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t},\,A^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t},\,\mu^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t},\zeta^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}_{t}\right)
be the unique solution to the McKean-Vlasov equation (
2.13
) with initial condition
\left(X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}\right)
. Let
\left(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},\,A_{t}^{M,\kappa},\,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right)
be the unique solution to the McKean-Vlasov equation (
2.21
) with the same initial conditions; then

(5.1)

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\,\,\mathcal{W}_{2,T}(\mu_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},\mu_{\cdot}^{M,\kappa})\,=\,0.

As a consequence,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\left|X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right|^{2}+\left|A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}-A_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right|^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,\quad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

As a consequence of the above result Proposition
2.9
readily follows.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

This is completely analogous to the proof of Proposition
2.7
, so we only sketch it.
From (
5.1
) and Proposition
3.1
, we obtain

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle-\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle\right|=0,\,\,\,\,

for any given
\Psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
which grows at most quadratically in
a\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, if we set
\Psi(x,a)=af(x)
,
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
and recalling (
2.18
) and (
2.24
) we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle af,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}af(x)\,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dxda)=\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}\rangle\,

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle af,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}af(x)\,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)=\langle f,\rho_{t}^{M,\kappa}\rangle.

From the above we obtain (
2.25
). The rest of the proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
2.7
. Then, from the uniqueness of the classical solution of (
2.19
) and of (
2.26
), the last statement of the proposition follows.
∎

The proof of Proposition
5.3
hinges on the preliminary estimates contained in Lemma
5.4
below, so we state this lemma first, and then prove it at the end of this section. In reading Lemma
5.4
note that equations (
2.17
) and (
2.23
) have the same structure, which is the structure of equation (
5.2
) below. That is, using the notation adopted in (
5.2
), we can write the solution
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
of (
2.17
) as
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}=\zeta_{t}^{(\mu^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})}
, while for
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
solution of (
2.23
) we have
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}=\zeta_{t}^{(\mu^{M,\kappa})}
. The crucial estimate in this section is the lower bound (
5.3
) below which allows, in the proof of Proposition
5.3
, to obtain the lower bound (
5.8
), which is uniform in
\varepsilon>0
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mu
be a fixed arbitrary path in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
, i.e.
\mu=\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))
and let
\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}
be the solution to the following PDE

(5.2)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}))\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right]\\
&\zeta^{(\mu)}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}),\end{cases}

where
\Gamma_{M}
has been defined in (
2.14
).
Then the following estimates hold:

(i)

For every
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

(5.3)

\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}(x)\geq e^{-t\,\,\mathcal{A}(M)}\zeta_{0}(x)\,,

where
\mathcal{A}(M)
is defined as

(5.4)

\mathcal{A}(M):=|V^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,.

(ii)

For any
t\in[0,T]
,

(5.5)

\left|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,t}\mathcal{H}(|\zeta_{0}|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},M,t)\,,

where
\mathcal{H}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous (in the product norm) locally bounded function such that
\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow[M\to\infty]{}+\infty
(for each fixed value of the first and third argument of the function).

(iii)

For any
t\in[0,T]
and any
\mu,\nu\in C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))
,

(5.6)

\left|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}\right|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\mathcal{G}(M,t)\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu_{s})\,ds\,,

where
\mathcal{G}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous function (in the product norm) such that
\mathcal{G}(M,t)\xrightarrow[M\to+\infty]{}+\infty
, for each fixed
t\in[0,T]
.

Note 5.5
.

Let us observe that the lower bound (
5.3
) does not depend on
\mu
. This is due to the fact that in the proof the only property of
\mu
we use is the fact that it is a probability measure for each
t
fixed, i.e.
\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\mu_{t}(dxda)=1
. In particular, when we use (
5.3
) to lower bound
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, this will result in a lower bound which is uniform in
\varepsilon>0
, as
\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dxda)=1
for every
\varepsilon,M,\kappa
and
t
, see (
5.8
) and the proof of Proposition
5.3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

We proceed in the same fashion as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
; to avoid notation overload, and since we focus on the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we omit the dependence on
M,\kappa
of the quantities used in the proof so that, in this proof only,
\left(X_{t}^{\varepsilon},A_{t}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon},\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}\right)
and
\left(X_{t},A_{t},\mu_{t},\zeta_{t}\right)
denote the solution to system (
2.13
) and (
2.21
), respectively. Moreover, throughout the proof
C
will be a generic constant, changing from line to line, depending continuously on the parameters in its argument, and increasing as a function of such parameters (e.g.
C(t,M)
will be a constant depending continuously on
t,M
and increasing in both arguments). With this premise, we start by estimating the difference
\left|X_{t}^{\varepsilon}-X_{t}\right|^{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X_{s}^{\varepsilon}-X_{s}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|X_{r}^{\varepsilon}-X_{r}\right|^{2}\,ds+2\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{r}^{\varepsilon})-\Gamma_{M}(X_{r},\mu_{r})\right|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{ineq gammma}}C(M)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|X_{r}^{\varepsilon}-X_{r}\right|^{2}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{r})\,ds\right]\,,

where we emphasize that
C(M)
is independent of
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence, from Gronwall’s lemma we obtain

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}^{\varepsilon}-X_{s}|^{2}\right]\,\leq C(t,M)\,\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu^{\varepsilon}_{r},\mu_{r})\,ds.

Moreover, using (
5.3
) applied to
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}
and
\zeta_{t}
, respectively, (see comments before the statement of Lemma
5.4
) we deduce the following lower bounds

(5.8)

\displaystyle\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)\geq\bar{C}(M,t,\eta),\qquad\mbox{for every }x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,\,t\in[0,T],

(5.9)

\displaystyle\zeta_{t}(x)\geq\hat{C}(M,t,\eta),\qquad\mbox{for every }x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,\,t\in[0,T],

where
\eta=\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\zeta_{0}>0
,
\bar{C},\hat{C}>0
for each fixed
M,t,\eta
and we emphasize that
\bar{C}
does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
i.e. the lower bound for
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}
holds uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
(see Note
5.5
).
From this and by recalling that
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx=1
we deduce

(5.10)

\left(\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq\bar{C}(M,t,\eta),\qquad\text{for every $x\in\mathbb{T}$ and $t\in[0,T]$,}

where the above lower bound holds uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{q(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})}\,-\,\frac{q(X_{t})}{\zeta_{t}(X_{t})}\right|^{2}\,=\,\left|\frac{q(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\,\zeta_{t}(X_{t})-q(X_{t})\,\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})}{\left(\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right)\,\zeta_{t}(X_{t})}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{lower bound zeta senza eps},\eqref{lower bound phi star zeta}}C(t,M,\eta)\left|q(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\,\zeta_{t}(X_{t})-q(X_{t})\,\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t,M,\eta)\left[\,\,\left|\left(q(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})-q(X_{t})\right)\,\zeta_{t}(X_{t})\right|^{2}+\left|q(X_{t})\,\left(\zeta_{t}(X_{t})-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right)\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C(t,M,\eta)\big[\,\,|\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\left|X_{t}^{\varepsilon}-X_{t}\right|^{2}+\left|\zeta_{t}(X_{t})-\zeta_{t}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left|\zeta_{t}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right|^{2}+\left|\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C(t,M,\eta)\big[\,\,|\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\left|X_{t}^{\varepsilon}-X_{t}\right|^{2}+\left|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\left|X_{t}-X_{t}^{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}

(5.11)

\displaystyle+\left|\zeta_{t}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\left|\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}-\zeta_{t}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\big],

where the above chain of inequalities holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
t\in[0,T]
and
C(t,M,\eta)
is a deterministic constant (depending on
\bar{C}
and
\hat{C}
).
Once this is in place, from the Sobolev embedding theorem we know that
H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
compactly embeds into
C(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
i.e. there exists a constant
c>0
such that

|f|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq c|f|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},\,\text{ for all $f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})$}\,.

From the above, by using
(ii)
and
(iii)
in Lemma
5.4
we obtain estimates for
|\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
,
|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
and
|\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}-\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
, respectively. Indeed, from
(ii)
in Lemma
5.4
applied to
\zeta_{t}
we obtain that

(5.12)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left[|\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\right]\leq\mathcal{H}\left(|\zeta_{0}|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},M,t\right)

while, from
(iii)
in Lemma
5.4
applied to
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}
and
\zeta_{t}
we deduce

(5.13)

|\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}-\zeta_{t}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\mathcal{G}(M,t)\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{s})\,ds\,.

From (
5.11
), (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) we deduce that the following holds for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

(5.14)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{q(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})}\,-\,\frac{q(X_{t})}{\zeta_{t}(X_{t})}\right|^{2}\leq C(t,M,\eta)\left[\left|X_{t}^{\varepsilon}-X_{t}\right|^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{s})\,ds+\left|\zeta_{t}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\right].

From Jensen’s inequality we then have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|A^{\varepsilon}_{s}-A_{s}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\,\,\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left|\frac{q(X_{s}^{\varepsilon})}{\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{s}^{\varepsilon}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon})}\,-\,\frac{q(X_{s})}{\zeta_{s}(X_{s})}\right|^{2}\left|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{KEM difference estimate}}C(t,M,\eta,\kappa)\left[\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}|X_{s}^{\varepsilon}-X_{s}|^{2}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{s})\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\left|\zeta_{t}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{t}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\right]

(5.15)

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{XKEM estimates}}C(t,M,\eta,\kappa)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu_{r}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{r})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\zeta_{r}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{r}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\right].

Hence, from the definition of 2-Wasserstein metric on
C_{t}
,
\mathcal{W}_{2,t}
(see Section
3
), we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{\cdot})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X^{\varepsilon}_{s}-X_{s}|^{2}+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|A^{\varepsilon}_{s}-A_{s}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{XKEM estimates},\eqref{AKEM estimates}}C(t,M,\eta,\kappa)\,\left[\,\,\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu^{\varepsilon}_{r},\mu_{r})\,ds\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\zeta_{r}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{r}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\right]\,

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{W2 leq W2t}}C(t,M,\eta,\kappa)\left[\,\,\,\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2,s}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{\cdot})\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\zeta_{r}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{r}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\right].

Therefore, from Gronwall’s inequality,

\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon},\mu_{\cdot})\leq C(t,M,\eta,\kappa)\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\zeta_{r}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{r}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds

By letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
and by recalling

\sup\limits_{r\in[0,T]}\left|\zeta_{r}-\Phi_{\varepsilon}*\zeta_{r}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\xrightarrow[\varepsilon\to 0]{}0

(see
(i)
of
[
24
, Theorem 6 in Appendix C]
) we can conclude and obtain the desired result thanks to the dominated convergence theorem (which can be applied in view of the bounds we obtained above on the
L^{\infty}
norm of
\zeta_{t}
and recalling that
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)dx=1
for every
\varepsilon>0
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

We show here how to prove point
i)
, which is the crucial one, the proofs of
(ii)
and
(iii)
can be found in Appendix
D
. For
i)
,
differentiating under the integral, we obtain

(5.16)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})\right|\leq^{\eqref{def_GammaM}}M|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\mu_{t}(dyda)=M|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

where we have used the fact that
\mu_{t}
is a probability measure for each
t
fixed. Using the above estimate, the lower bound (
5.3
) can be readily deduced from Theorem
B.4
.
∎

Note 5.6
.

Before moving on to the next section we want to clarify why we have opted for an upper bound of the type (
5.16
), which produces the lower bound (
5.3
). First of all, note that at this stage in the proof we are only aiming at a lower bound on
\zeta_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
which is uniform in
\varepsilon
, we do not need a lower bound uniform in
M
, as
M
is fixed. And indeed the lower bound (
5.3
) is uniform in
\varepsilon
but not in
M
. One may wonder whether it is possible, with easy tricks, to obtain a lower abound which is uniform in both parameters in one go (so that both limits could be taken together). We have not been able to do so and to explain why this is the case it is useful to point out the following: one may try to upper bound the quantity on the LHS of (
5.16
) differently, for example as follows

\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})\right|\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|\chi_{M}(a)F^{(n+1)}(x-y)|\mu_{t}(dyda)\leq|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}(dyda)\,.

By doing so
M
does not appear explicitly, but now the quantity on the RHS of the above would have to be controlled in the proofs with
\mu_{t}=\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
. That is, we would have to control a term of the form

(5.17)

\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|\chi_{M}(a)F^{(n+1)}(x-y)|\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dyda)\leq|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(dyda)\,,

and the RHS of (
5.17
) does depend on
M
.
Because of the (complicated) equation for
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
we have not been able to control the above uniformly in
\varepsilon
and
M
simultaneously, so we take the approach of producing estimates which are uniform in “one parameter at a time", the relevant one for the step at hand. The need to control a term of the form (
5.17
) will arise again in the next step, when letting
M\rightarrow\infty
, see (
6.7
).
∎

6.
The Limit
M\to\infty

In this section we prove Proposition
2.11
, i.e. the convergence of
\rho^{M,\kappa}_{t}
to
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
, as
M\to\infty
. Analogously to previous sections, to do so, we show that for any given
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
the law
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
of the pair
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa})
, solution to (
2.21
), converges to
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
, law of the pair
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
in (
2.28
), as
M\to\infty
. This is the result of Proposition
6.4
which implies Proposition
2.11
. Therefore, we focus on proving Proposition
6.4
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
be as in Definition
4.1
and Assumption
2.2
. The 4-tuple
t\to(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{\kappa})\in C([0,T];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
is a solution to (
2.28
) if the following conditions hold:

(i)

(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
is
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
-adapted;

(ii)

The stochastic process
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
satisfies (
2.28
), for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., where
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
and
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
is the unique classic solution to (
2.29
).

Lemma 6.2
.

For any given
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, system (
2.28
) admits a unique solution in the sense of Definition
6.1
. Furthermore, for any given
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
the function
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
is strictly positive, i.e.
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}(x)>0
for every
x\in\mathbb{T}
,
t\in[0,T]
and the pair
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
belongs to the space
L^{2}\left(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})\right)
.

We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for the proof of this lemma. As mentioned in Section
2
, for some of the proofs of this section it will be convenient to work with the dynamics (
2.28
)-(
2.29
) in its equivalent form (
2.34
). Hence we clarify in Note
6.3
why such equivalence holds.

Note 6.3
.

Let us first consider a solution to (
2.28
) and then define
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
as in (
2.31
); one can see that the measure
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
is a weak measure-valued solution to (
2.33
). A posteriori, from the smoothing properties of (
2.33
) - see e.g. Lemma
A.1
-
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
turns out to be a classical solution as well i.e.
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.
12
12

12

Note that (
2.33
) is non-linear, while (
2.19
) and (
2.26
) are linear; so proving that a measure-valued solution to (
2.33
) with smooth initial datum is a smooth function is a bit more delicate.
Moreover, (
2.33
) is a closed equation in
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
(this was not true for (
2.19
) nor for (
2.26
)). Since, in view of (
2.32
), the PDE (
2.29
) solved by
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
can be rewritten as

(6.1)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{t}^{\kappa})\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}\right],\\
&\zeta^{\kappa}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0},\end{cases}

by putting together (
2.28
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
) and (
6.1
) the formulation in terms of (
2.34
) readily follows.

To prove the converse: let us then consider system (
2.34
) and define
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}:=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
where
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa})
is the pair in (
2.34
). It is easy to see that
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
. If we now set
\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\kappa}(dx):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)
then
\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\kappa}(dx)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
is a weak measure-valued solution to the PDE (
2.33
) with initial datum
\tilde{\rho}_{0}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a\mu_{0}(x,a)da=\rho_{0}(x)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. From the smoothing properties of (
2.33
) (see again Lemma
A.1
) we obtain that
\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\kappa}
is a classical solution and, therefore, from the uniqueness it thus coincides with
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
.
By now using (
2.32
) in the opposite direction, that is, from right to left, it is easy to go from system (
2.34
) to system (
2.28
).
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed and let
X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
be as in Definition
6.1
. Let us consider the stochastic processes
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
and
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{\kappa})
solutions to (
2.21
) and (
2.28
), respectively (with the same initial conditions). Then, for every
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, the following limit holds:

\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{W}_{2,T}(\mu_{\cdot}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{\cdot}^{\kappa})=0.

As a consequence,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\left|X_{t}^{M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}+\left|A_{t}^{M,\kappa}-A_{t}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,\quad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

Proposition
2.11
follows as a straightforward corollary of the above result.

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

The proof can be done in the exact same way of the proof of Proposition
2.9
so we don’t repeat it.
∎

In this section and the next we will make use of the heat semigroup
\{e^{t\partial_{xx}}\}_{t\geq 0}
which we recall is the family of linear operators from
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
defined as

(6.2)

e^{t\partial_{xx}}f=G_{t}^{\text{per}}*f,\qquad f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}),t\geq 0,

where
G_{t}^{\text{per}}
(see
[
4
]
) denotes the fundamental solution of the heat equation on
\mathbb{T}
.
Furthermore, by letting
\{e_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
defined as

(6.3)

e_{z}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sin(zx),\qquad z>0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\qquad z=0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\cos(zx),\qquad z<0,\end{cases}

one can readily see that (
6.2
) acts on each element of (
6.3
) as follows

(6.4)

e^{t\partial_{xx}}e_{z}(x)=e^{-t|z|^{2}}e_{z}(x),\qquad t\geq 0,\,x\in\mathbb{T}.

Lastly, we use the notation
\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathcal{U})}_{t}
to denote the following convolution

(6.5)

\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}d\mathcal{U}_{s}(x),\qquad t\geq 0,\,x\in\mathbb{T}\,,

where
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is the external forcing term appearing in (
1.1
). Furthermore, if
\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)=q(x)Z_{t}
with
q
as in Assumption
2.1
and
Z_{\cdot}
a time-dependent path (we emphasize, dependent on time only, not on space as well), either piece-wise
C^{1}
or
\gamma
-Hölder continuous for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
; to denote the convolution (
6.5
) in this case we use the notation
\mathcal{Q}^{(Z)}_{t}
instead (i.e. the spatial coefficient
q(x)
in the superscript is omitted). For instance, in Lemma
6.5
below the term
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}
is defined as
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}q(x)\,dY^{\kappa}_{s}
.

Let us also point out that in (
6.5
) the integral
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}
is well-defined as a stochastic Itô integral when
\mathcal{U}_{t}=W_{t}
is a trace-class Wiener noise, while, when
\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)=q(x)Z_{t}
it is defined in the Riemann sense if
Z_{t}
is piece-wise
C^{1}
and in the Young sense if
Z_{t}
is Hölder continuous.

We now state a series of lemmata, which will be needed to prove Proposition
6.4
. We then prove Proposition
6.4
and postpone the proof of the lemmata to the end of the section.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
; then (for each
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
) the PDE (
2.33
) admits a unique time-continuous and spatially-smooth solution, i.e.
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\in C^{0,\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.
13
13

13

The space
C^{0,\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
denotes the class of real-valued functions continuous w.r.t the variable
t\in[0,T]
and smooth w.r.t the variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
.

Furthermore, the following estimate on the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
and
\partial_{x}\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
holds:

(6.6)

\left|\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left|\partial_{x}\rho_{s}^{\kappa}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\leq\mathcal{J}\left(\left|\rho_{0}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2},\left|\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))}\right)

where
\mathcal{J}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a locally bounded continuous function.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let the stochastic process
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
be the solution to (
2.21
) with initial condition
X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
as in Definition
6.1
. Then for every
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
the following estimate for the first moment (in the variable
a\in\mathbb{R}
) of
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
holds:

(6.7)

\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{0}(dxda)+\int_{0}^{t}\,ds|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|\int_{\mathbb{T}}|q(x)|\,dx.

Lemma
6.7
below (which hinges on Lemma
6.6
) provides lower and upper bounds on
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
which are, crucially, independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Next, Lemma
6.8
states lower and upper bounds on
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
. Moreover in
(iv)
and
(v)
of Lemma
6.8
we prove that (
4.7
) and (
5.6
), when applied with
\mu_{\cdot}=\mu_{\cdot}^{M,\kappa}
and
\nu_{\cdot}=\mu_{\cdot}^{\kappa}
, hold with constants which are independent of
M
. In other words, (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) are specific instances of (
4.7
) and (
5.6
), the difference being that here we need to show that the constants on the RHS of (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) do not depend on
M
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
be the solution to (
2.21
) with initial condition
(X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})
as in Definition
6.1
. Then
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is point-wise bounded from below, uniformly in
M\in\mathbb{N}
and, moreover, the
H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is bounded from above, uniformly in
M\in\mathbb{N}
. That is,

(6.8)

\displaystyle\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(x)\geq C_{1},

(6.9)

\displaystyle|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq C_{2},

where the constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
C_{1}
is independent of
x\in\mathbb{T}
(but both depend continuously on
t
).

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
T>0
be fixed and let
(X_{t}^{M,\kappa},A_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
and
(X_{t}^{\kappa},A_{t}^{\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{\kappa})
be the solutions (up to time
T
) to (
2.21
) and (
2.28
), respectively, with the same initial conditions
(X_{0},A_{0})
,
\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
as in Definition
6.1
. Let us also consider the weighted distribution
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
of
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
in (
2.31
), whose density
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
solves the PDE (
2.33
).
Then the following estimates hold:

(i)

For every
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
t\in[0,T]
the following point-wise lower bound for
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
holds

\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}(x)\geq e^{-t\mathcal{V}(\kappa,\rho^{\kappa})}\zeta_{0}(x),

where
\mathcal{V}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\kappa,\rho^{\kappa})

\displaystyle:=|V^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,T]}|\rho_{r}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}}+|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,T]}|\rho_{r}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.

(ii)

The following estimate for the
H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}
holds for every
t\in[0,T]
:

|\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\mathcal{N}\left(|\zeta_{0}|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}|\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},t\right),

where
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous (in the product norm) function.

(iii)

There exists a constant
\bar{K}=\bar{K}(n)>0
(dependent on
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
) such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

(6.10)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|^{n}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)\leq K(n).

(iv)

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{T}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(6.11)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(y,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\right|^{2}\,\leq C(n)(\,|x-y|^{2}\,+\,\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})),

where
C(n):=\max\left\{\bar{K}(1),1\right\}|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
with
\bar{K}(1)
the constant in (
6.10
) for
n=1
so that
C(n)
is independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
.

(v)

There exists a constant
\hat{K}=\hat{K}(t,\kappa)>0
independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that the inequality below holds for every
t\in[0,T]
:

(6.12)

|\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\hat{K}(t,\kappa)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\,ds+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\right].

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

We proceed in the same fashion of the proof of Proposition
5.3
. So some steps are explained more quickly here and we give more details on the part of computation that differs from the proof of Proposition
5.3
. In what follows we denote by
C(t)
a constant depending continuously on
t
which may change from line to line. Estimating
\mathbb{E}\,|X_{t}^{M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{\kappa}|^{2}
gives us

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X_{s}^{M,\kappa}-X_{s}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|X_{r}^{M,\kappa}-X_{r}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}\,ds\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})\right|^{2}\,ds\right],

so from Gronwall’s lemma we deduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X_{s}^{M,\kappa}-X_{s}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}\leq C(t)\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})\right|^{2}\,ds.

Let us turn our attention to the RHS of the above inequality:

\displaystyle|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left[|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa})-\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})-\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{ineq gammma 2}}C\left[\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})+|X_{r}^{M,\kappa}-X_{r}^{\kappa}|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|^{2}\right]

(6.13)

\displaystyle\leq C\left[\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})+|X_{r}^{M,\kappa}-X_{r}^{\kappa}|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{r}^{\kappa}(dxda)\right],\qquad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

To obtain the third addend in (
6.13
) we have applied Jensen’s inequality to the term
|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|^{2}
combined with the fact that the normalization constant
\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}
of the measure
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
is bounded from above by 1 uniformly in
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
and
t>0
. Namely, if we let
\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)
and notice that
\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}\leq 1
, uniformly in
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
and
t>0
then we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\Gamma_{M}(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})-\Gamma(X_{r}^{\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right)^{2}\left(\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}aF^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{\kappa}-y)\frac{\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dyda)}{\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\left|F^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{t}^{\kappa}-y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dyda)}{\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{M,\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dyda).

This gives the third addend in (
6.13
).
Hence, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X_{s}^{M,\kappa}-X_{s}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t)\Bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|X_{r}^{M,\kappa}-X_{r}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\,ds

\displaystyle+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\Bigg],

and again from Gronwall’s lemma we conclude

(6.14)

\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\left|X_{s}^{M,\kappa}-X_{s}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}\leq C(t)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\,ds+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\right].

As for the difference
\mathbb{E}\left|A_{t}^{M,\kappa}-A_{t}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}
we can proceed similarly to what we did in (
5.14
) (we recall that
\eta=\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\zeta_{0}
and
C(t,\kappa,\eta)
is a constant, depending continuously on
t,\kappa
and
\eta
, which may change from line to line):

\displaystyle\left|\frac{q(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})}{\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})}\,-\,\frac{q(X_{t}^{\kappa})}{\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}(X_{t})}\right|^{2}\,=\,\left|\frac{q(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})\,\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}(X_{t}^{\kappa})-q(X_{t}^{\kappa})\,\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})}{\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})\,\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}(X_{t}^{\kappa})}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t,\kappa,\eta)\left[|\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\left|X_{t}^{M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}+\left|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\left|X_{t}^{\kappa}-X_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right|^{2}+\left|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\right],

By means of
(ii)
combined with estimate (
6.6
) for
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
and
(v)
in Lemma
6.8
we then deduce

\displaystyle\left|\frac{q(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})}{\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})}\,-\,\frac{q(X_{t}^{\kappa})}{\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}(X_{t}^{\kappa})}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t,\kappa,\eta)\left[\left|X_{t}^{M,\kappa}-X_{t}^{\kappa}\right|^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\,ds\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\,ds\right].

Hence, similarly to (
4.11
), by using the above along with (
6.14
) we obtain

(6.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|A^{M,\kappa}_{s}-A_{s}^{\kappa}|^{2}\right]\,

\displaystyle\leq C(t,\kappa,\eta)\left[\,\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu_{r}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{r}^{\kappa})|\partial_{s}Y^{\kappa}|^{2}\,ds\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\partial_{t}Y^{\kappa}|^{2}\right].\,\,

From (
6.14
), (
