6.15
), the definition (
3.2
) of 2-Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2,t}
with
\mathcal{X}=C_{t}
and (
3.5
) we deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu^{M,\kappa},\mu^{\kappa})\leq C(t,\kappa,\eta)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2,s}^{2}(\mu^{M,\kappa},\mu^{\kappa})\,ds+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\right],

and, from Gronwall’s inequality,

\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu^{M,\kappa},\mu^{\kappa})\leq C(t,\kappa,\eta)\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda).

By letting
M\to\infty
and using
(iii)
in Lemma
6.8
we obtain desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

The well-posedness in mild sense of (
2.33
) follows from Lemma
A.1
. It remains to show that
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
is actually a smooth function w.r.t. the spatial variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
. To achieve this, we note that the coefficients in the PDE (
2.33
) are smooth, so that the improved regularity in the spatial variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
can be done by following the steps of
[
10
, Theorem 4.2]
so we don’t repeat them. We just highlight that the proof of
[
10
, Theorem 4.2]
goes through provided one can control the
L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of the solution itself. In the setting of
[
10
]
, due to the gradient structure of PDE at hand, the
L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm estimate on the solution was obtained by using a non-negative initial datum and by the positivity-preserving property of the evolution; this allowed the authors of
[
10
]
to conclude that
|\rho_{t}|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=|\rho_{0}|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
for
t\geq 0
(see
[
10
, Corollary 2.2]
). In our case, since our PDE is perturbed by the additive term
q(x)\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}
, there is no such thing as mass preservation and preservation of positivity. So, in order to use the scheme of proof of
[
10
, Theorem 4.2]
, we need an estimate on the
L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
. This can be obtained following the steps of
[
2
, Proposition B.1]
, so we don’t repeat the whole argument and just conclude that
|\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
satisfies, for
t\geq 0
, the following estimate:

|\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq|\rho_{0}|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|q|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|\,ds\,.

The estimate (
6.6
) can be obtained by following the steps of the proof of
[
2
, Proposition B.1]
.
∎

Note 6.9
.

We recall that from Proposition
2.14
we know that the pair
(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
is the unique solution to the system of PDEs (
2.22
)-(
2.23
). In particular, we know that
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is a weak measure-valued solution to (
2.22
) while
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, due to the smoothing properties of (
2.23
) (see e.g.
[
27
]
and
[
24
, Chapter 7]
), is the unique classical solution to (
2.23
).

If we define the measure
\tilde{\zeta}_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dx):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)
, a straightforward calculation shows that such a measure is a weak measure-valued solution to (
2.23
) with initial datum
\zeta_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{0}(x,a)da
. From
3
in Assumption
2.2
we know that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{0}(x,a)da=\zeta_{0}(x)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. Hence, again from the smoothing properties of (
2.23
) one can see that
\tilde{\zeta}_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is actually a classical solution to (
2.23
) and it thus coincides with
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
.

As a result of the above, we have obtained that
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
can be viewed as a the
\mathbb{T}
-marginal of
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
. Hence,
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{M,\kappa})
and, therefore,

(6.16)

\displaystyle\left\langle f,\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left(f\left(X_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right)\right),\qquad\text{for any $f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})$}.

The expression (
6.16
) can of course be written in terms of the measure
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
, that is,

\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}f(x)\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda),\qquad\text{for any $f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})$}.

In particular, since
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
is a deterministic trajectory and
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}>0
, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
t\in[0,T]
(from Lemma
5.2
), this implies that
\frac{1}{\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}}
can be taken as a test function in the above equality. Hence, for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
we have

(6.17)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}f(x)\frac{1}{\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(x)}\,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda).

The equality (
6.17
) is crucial to the proof of Lemma
6.6
and to our overall strategy.

Had we not fixed the noise but considered the particle system (
2.3
) with a Brownian motion
w_{t}
in place of
Y_{t}^{\kappa}
, the joint distribution, say
\mu_{t}^{M}
, which would appear at this point in the proof, would be stochastic, hence its marginal
\zeta_{t}^{M}
would be stochastic as well, and adapted to the filtration
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
. Therefore, in this context, equality (
6.17
) would hold with
\mu_{t}^{M}
and
\zeta_{t}^{M}
in place of
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
and
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
for every realisation
\bar{\omega}
belonging to the set defined as
\bar{\Omega}_{\zeta^{M}}:=\left\{\bar{\omega}\in\Omega\,|\,\zeta_{t}^{M}(\bar{\omega})>0,\,\text{for all $t\in[0,T]$ and $x\in\mathbb{T}$}\right\}\in\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{T}
. This produces measurability issues at various stages of the proof. This is another step where working deterministically helps.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

By recalling that
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is the weak solution to the PDE (
2.22
) we obtain that for any
\Psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
the following holds:

(6.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left\langle\Psi,\mu_{0}\right\rangle\right\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\left\langle\partial_{xx}\Psi+(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa})\partial_{x}\Psi,\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle\,ds

(6.19)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\left\langle\frac{q}{\zeta_{s}^{M,\kappa}}\partial_{a}\Psi,\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa}\right\rangle\right\rangle dY_{s}^{\kappa}.

Moreover, since we know from Lemma
5.2
that
t\to\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))
then it is easy to deduce that the above equality can be extended
to any
\Psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
which grows at most quadratically in
a\in\mathbb{R}
along with its derivatives. To obtain (
6.7
), it suffices to take as test function
\Psi(x,a)=|a|
. Unfortunately, such test function is not differentiable in
a\in\mathbb{R}
. This minor issue can be overcome by considering
\Psi_{\delta}(x,a)=\sqrt{a^{2}+\delta}
,
x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
, doing all the calculations with
\Psi_{\delta}
in place of
\Psi
and then set
\delta\downarrow 0
. For the sake of clarity, in what follows, we just work with
\Psi(x,a)=|a|
(knowing that all the calculations should be done for
\Psi_{\delta}
and we don’t show explicitly the step
\delta\downarrow 0
). From (
6.18
) and (
6.19
) with
\Psi(x,a)=|a|
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{0}(dxda)+\int_{0}^{t}\,dY_{s}^{\kappa}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\frac{a}{|a|}\frac{q(x)}{\zeta_{s}^{M,\kappa}(x)}\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa}(dxda).

However, since
dY_{s}^{\kappa}=\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}ds
(
Y_{t}^{\kappa}
is a piece-wise
C^{1}
in time
t
) and by using the fact that
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}
is a probability measure we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{0}(dxda)+\int_{0}^{t}\,ds|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\frac{a}{|a|}\frac{q(x)}{\zeta_{s}^{M,\kappa}(x)}\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{fondamentale}}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{0}(dxda)+\int_{0}^{t}\,ds|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|\int_{\mathbb{T}}|q(x)|\,dx,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

Recalling that
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
solves the PDE (
2.17
), from (
6.7
) and Theorem
B.4
we deduce

\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}(x)\geq e^{-t\mathcal{E}(M)}\zeta_{0}(x),

where
\mathcal{E}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(M)

\displaystyle:=|V^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\left|\Gamma_{M}(\cdot,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\left|\Gamma_{M}(\cdot,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

From the above, an upper bound for
\mathcal{E}
(and consequently a lower bound for
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}
) independent of
M\in\mathbb{N}
can be easily derived. Indeed, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(M)

\displaystyle\leq|V^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\left(\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}\right)^{2}+|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},

and recalling (
6.7
), the lower bound (
6.8
) easily follows.
As for (
6.9
) we proceed with same method adopted to obtain
(ii)
in Lemma
5.4
so we don’t redo the whole proof, we only point out the main difference. In that proof we used upper bound (
5.16
) on
\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}
. That bound is not sufficient here, as it would depend on
M
.
To overcome this issue, we use upper bound (
6.7
) instead, to estimate
\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})
:

\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\right|\leq|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa}(dxda)\leq^{\eqref{super}}C,

with
C
independent of
M
(but dependent on
T
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.8
.

The lower bound
(i)
and the estimate
(ii)
can be proven similarly to
(i)
and
(ii)
in Lemma
5.4
, using the formulation (
2.34
) of (
2.28
), so we don’t repeat the proof.
The upper bound
(iii)
follows from the equation of
A_{t}^{\kappa}
in (
2.34
) and from
(i)
. Indeed, from Jensen’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|^{n}\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)=\mathbb{E}(|A_{t}^{\kappa}|^{n})\leq t^{n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{|q(X_{s}^{\kappa})|^{n}}{|\zeta_{s}^{\kappa}(X_{s}^{\kappa})|^{n}}|\partial_{s}Y^{\kappa}|^{n}\,ds,

which gives
(iii)
. As for
(iv)
, we proceed in the same way we have proven (
4.7
) in Lemma
4.4
.
Hence, from the triangle inequality and the Lipschitz continuity of
F^{(n+1)}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(y,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\right|+\left|\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})-\partial_{x}^{n}\Gamma_{M}(y,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\right|

\displaystyle\leq|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa})+|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|x-y|\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{M}(a)\,\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)

\displaystyle\leq|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa})+|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|x-y|\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda)

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{W1 leq W2}}|F^{(n+1)}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\max\left\{\,\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}|a|\,\mu_{t}^{\kappa}(dxda),1\right\}\left(|x-y|+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\right).

From (
6.10
) and the above estimate,
(iv)
follows.
As for
(v)
we refer the reader to Appendix
D
for its proof.
∎

7.
The limit
\kappa\to\infty

The purpose of this section is to prove Proposition
2.12
. We start with some preliminary observations.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
and
Z\in C^{\gamma}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
with
\gamma\in(0,1)
; let us define the Young integral
\mathcal{I}_{f}(t,x)
as

\mathcal{I}_{f}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}f_{s}(x)\,dZ_{s},\qquad t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{T}.

Then
\mathcal{I}_{f}(t,x)\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
and its weak derivative is given by
\mathcal{I}_{\partial_{x}f}
. That is,

(7.1)

\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\mathcal{I}_{f}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}f_{s}(x)\,dZ_{s},\,\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,\,\,t\in[0,T].

and, moreover, the following estimate holds

(7.2)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\mathcal{I}_{f}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma}\max\{T,1\}\big(|f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle+|\partial_{x}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|\partial_{x}\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\big)|Z_{\cdot}|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}},

where
|\cdot|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}}
is the semi-norm introduced in (
C.1
) with
E=\mathbb{R}
.

The proof of Lemma
7.1
can be found in Appendix
C
.
If we now use Lemma
7.1
with
Z_{t}=Y_{t}
(we recall that
Y_{t}\in C^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
) and
f_{t}(x)=e^{t\partial_{xx}}q(x)
, then from (
7.2
) we obtain that
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
for any given
t\geq 0
. Furthermore, from the semi-group property of the heat semi-group (
6.4
) we get
t\to\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
.

Similarly, applying Lemma
7.1
to the family of piece-wise
C^{1}
functions
\{Y_{\cdot}^{\kappa}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
approximating
Y_{t}
in
C^{\gamma}
(with
\gamma<\alpha
) we obtain that
\left\{\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
, uniformly with respect to
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, from (
7.2
) applied with
Z_{t}=Y_{t}^{\kappa}
and
\gamma<\alpha
we obtain

(7.3)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma}\max\{T,1\}|q|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|Y_{\cdot}^{\kappa}|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}},\qquad\text{for every $\kappa\in\mathbb{N}$},

and from (
7.2
) applied to
Z_{t}=Y_{t}-Y_{t}^{\kappa}
and
\gamma<\alpha
we obtain

(7.4)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}-\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{\gamma}\max\{T,1\}|q|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|Y_{\cdot}-Y_{\cdot}^{\kappa}|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}},\qquad\text{for every $\kappa\in\mathbb{N}$}.

Since we have assumed that
Y_{\cdot}^{\kappa}\xrightarrow{\kappa\to\infty}Y_{\cdot}
in
C^{\gamma}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma<\alpha
, from (
7.3
) it follows that there exists
\bar{\mathcal{C}}>0
independent of
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup\limits_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\left|Y_{\cdot}^{\kappa}\right|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}}\leq\bar{\mathcal{C}}
and, consequently,

(7.5)

\sup\limits_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{\gamma}\bar{\mathcal{C}}\max\{T,1\}|q|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.

With this observation in place, since the function
\mathcal{J}
in (
6.6
) is locally bounded, from (
7.5
) we have

(7.6)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left[|\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|\partial_{x}\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\right]\leq\sup\limits_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{J}\left(|\rho_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2},\left|\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))}\right)\leq\hat{\mathcal{C}},

for some
\hat{\mathcal{C}}>0
independent of
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
.
We can now prove Proposition
2.12
.

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
then from Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\kappa}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}-\langle f,\rho_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\right|\leq|f|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\rho_{t}^{\kappa}-\rho_{t}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.

Hence, if we prove that the following limit holds

(7.7)

\lim\limits_{\kappa\to\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}^{\kappa}-\rho_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=0,

then the proof of Proposition
2.12
is concluded.

To prove (
7.7
), let us first recall the following standard heat kernel estimate (the proof of which can be found in
[
2
, Lemma 4.1]
): for every
f\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
we have

(7.8)

\left|\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}\partial_{x}f_{s}ds\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq\hat{C}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|f_{s}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds,\qquad t\in[0,T],

where
\hat{C}>0
is a constant independent of
f
.
We now recall (see Lemma
A.1
) that the mild and weak solution of (
2.33
) coincide, and the same is true for equation (
1.3
). In this proof, we use the mild formulations of both equations (but we could have done it using the weak formulation as well). From the mild formulation of (
1.3
) and (
2.33
) and the estimate (
7.8
) we have (in what follows
C
denotes a generic constant independent of
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
but possibly dependent on
T
, which may change from line to line)

\displaystyle|\rho_{t}-\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}-\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+C\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|V^{{}^{\prime}}(\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa})|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds

\displaystyle+C\int\limits_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|(F^{{}^{\prime}}\ast\rho_{s})\rho_{s}-(F^{{}^{\prime}}\ast\rho_{s}^{\kappa})\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}-\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+C\int\limits_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds

\displaystyle+C\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|(F^{{}^{\prime}}\ast(\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}))\rho_{s}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds

\displaystyle+C\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|(F^{{}^{\prime}}\ast\rho_{s}^{\kappa})(\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa})|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds,

where we recall that
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{\left(Y^{\kappa}\right)}
are defined by (
6.5
) with
Z_{t}=Y_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, and
Z_{t}=Y_{t}^{\kappa}
,
t\geq 0
, respectively.
Since
\rho_{t}
is the solution to (
1.3
) (which has the same structure of the PDE (
2.33
) solved by
\rho_{t}^{\kappa}
) with the same strategy adopted for obtaining (
6.6
) one can see that the exact estimate (
6.6
) holds for
\rho_{t}
as well so that there exists
\tilde{C}=\tilde{C}(T)>0
for which
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq\tilde{C}
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left(F^{{}^{\prime}}\ast\rho_{s}\right)\left(\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}\right)\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq C

\displaystyle|\rho_{s}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq C\cdot\tilde{C}|\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},

and, similarly, using (
7.6
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\left(F^{{}^{\prime}}\ast(\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa})\right)\rho_{s}^{\kappa}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,\leq\,C\cdot\hat{C}|\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},

for all
s\in[0,T]
.
From the above, we then have

\begin{split}|\rho_{t}-\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}&\leq\,C\left[\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}-\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+(\hat{C}+\tilde{C})\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\rho_{s}-\rho_{s}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds\right],\end{split}

so that using Generalised Gronwall’s lemma inequality (see e.g
[
2
, Lemma 4.3]
) finally gives

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}-\rho_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq\bar{C}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y)}-\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(Y^{\kappa})}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

where
\bar{C}>0
depends on
|V^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
,
|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
,
|q|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
,
\hat{C}
and
\tilde{C}
.
Now using (
7.4
) and by recalling that
|Y-Y^{\kappa}|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}}\xrightarrow{\kappa\to\infty}0
the assertion readily follows.
∎

8.
Proof of Theorem
2.6

The purpose of this section is to prove Theorem
2.6
. This is obtained by first proving Proposition
8.4
and then Corollary
8.5
. We start by considering the following class of PDEs:

(8.1)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho^{\infty}_{t}=\partial_{xx}\rho_{t}^{\infty}+\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F*\rho^{\infty}_{t})\rho^{\infty}_{t}]+\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{z},\qquad t\in(0,T],\\
&\rho^{\infty}|_{t=0}=\rho_{0},\end{cases}

where
\{e_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the basis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
defined in (
6.3
),
\{\lambda_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
is as in (
1.2
), lastly,
\{Y_{\cdot}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a family of
\alpha
-Hölder time-continuous paths,
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Analogously to what we have done in previous sections, this will allow to treat deterministically equation (
1.5
), which, by using (
1.2
), can be rewritten as

(8.2)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}v_{t}=\partial_{xx}v_{t}+\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F*v_{t})v_{t}]+\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}\partial_{t}w_{t}^{z},\,\,\,\,t\in(0,T],\\
&v|_{t=0}=v_{0}\end{cases}

where
\{w_{\cdot}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
is as in (
1.2
). The well-posedness of the SPDE (
1.5
) is stated in Lemma
A.1
and it is a consequence of the lemma below, which is a straightforward re-adaptation to our context, of
[
16
, Theorem 2.13]
.

Lemma 8.1
.

[
16
, Theorem 2.13]

Let the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
in (
1.2
) satisfy the following condition:

(8.3)

\text{$\exists\,\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)$ such that }\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}^{2}|z|^{4\delta}<+\infty;

then the (stochastic) convolution
t\to\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(W)}
belongs to
C\left([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)
,
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. and

\hat{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(W)}\right|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\right)<+\infty.

Let us also point out that from (
8.3
) and by following the steps of the proof of
[
16
, Theorem 2.13]
one also obtains

(8.4)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{L\to\infty}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\sum\limits_{|z|\geq L}z^{2}\lambda_{z}^{2}\left|\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}\,dw_{s}^{z}\right|^{2}=0,\qquad\hat{\mathbb{P}}-a.s.

We refer the reader to Appendix
D
for the proof of (
8.4
).
Since our aim is to study (
8.2
) through deterministic systems of type (
8.1
), from Lemma
8.1
and (
8.4
) we impose the following conditions on the Hölder time-continuous trajectories
Y_{t}^{z}
in (
8.1
):

•

The convolution
\mathcal{Y}_{\cdot}
defined as

(8.5)

\mathcal{Y}_{t}(x):=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(x)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}\,dY_{s}^{z},\qquad t\in[0,T],\,\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T},

is a time-continuous
H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-valued process. That is,
\mathcal{\mathcal{Y}}_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
.

•

The following condition holds

(8.6)

\lim\limits_{L\to\infty}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\sum\limits_{|z|\geq L}z^{2}\lambda_{z}^{2}\left|\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}\,dY_{s}^{z}\right|^{2}=0.

Condition (
8.6
) is a technical condition needed to prove the limit
m\to\infty
and it becomes clear in the proof of Proposition
8.4
why we have imposed it on
\{Y_{\cdot}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
14
14

14

Let us point out that Lemma
8.1
and (
8.4
) show that set given by the family of trajectories
\{Y_{\cdot}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
for which the convolution
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
defined as in (
8.5
) belongs to
C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
and that satisfies condition (
8.6
) is non-empty. Note moreover that since
\mathcal{Y}_{\cdot}
is well-defined in
C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
, condition (
8.6
), without the supremum on
[0,T]
, is automatically true. Hence, (
8.6
) is an extra condition because of the presence of the supremum.

The well-posedness of the PDE (
8.1
) can be found in Lemma
A.1
and it follows from the lemma below.

Lemma 8.2
.

The convolution
\mathcal{Y}_{\cdot}
defined in (
8.5
) belongs to
C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
and its weak derivative is given by

(8.7)

\partial_{x}\mathcal{Y}=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}|z|\lambda_{z}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}e_{-z}(x)\,dY_{s}^{z},\,\,\,\,t\in[0,T],\,\,\,\,dx-a.e.

Proof.

We just have to show (
8.7
). Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
be a test function then from Fubini-Tonelli’s theorem (which can be applied since
\mathcal{Y}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
) we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y},\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}\,dY_{s}^{z}\langle e_{z},\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}|z|\lambda_{z}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}\,dY_{s}^{z}\langle e_{-z},\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=-\left\langle\,\,\,\,\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}|z|\lambda_{z}e_{-z}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)z^{2}}\,dY_{s}^{z},\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},

which implies the statement.
∎

To prove Theorem
2.6
we begin with observing the following fact: we already know that Theorem
2.4
holds; therefore, to conclude it suffices to extend Theorem
2.4
to a sum of infinitely many Brownian motions. To start, we know that Proposition
2.5
allows us to extend Theorem
2.4
to a sum of finitely many Brownian motions. Hence, to conclude only the limit
m\to\infty
remains to be shown. With this direction in mind, we first prove a preliminary result, Lemma
8.3
, which provides us a uniform upper bound in
m\in\mathbb{N}
on the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\rho_{t}^{m}
so that the limit
m\to\infty
in the deterministic setting can be taken care of, this is done in Proposition
8.4
. After that, we state and prove Corollary
8.5
and at last demonstrate Theorem
2.6
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\rho_{t}^{m}
be the mild solution to (
2.11
); then the following holds:

\sup\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}^{m}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}<+\infty.

Proof.

From
[
2
, Section 4]
we know that

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}^{m}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{x}\rho_{s}^{m}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds\leq\mathcal{J}\left(|\rho_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2},\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\mathcal{Y}^{m}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\right),

where
\mathcal{J}
is the same function of estimate (
6.6
) and
\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m}
is defined as

(8.8)

\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m}(x):=\sum\limits_{|z|\leq m}\lambda_{z}e_{z}(x)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)|z|^{2}}\,dY_{s}^{z},\qquad t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{T},\,m\in\mathbb{N}.

Furthermore, since
\mathcal{Y}_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
we obtain

\sup\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}<+\infty

from which the statement readily follows.
∎

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
\rho_{t}^{m}
be the solution to the PDE (
2.11
) and let
\rho_{t}^{\infty}
be the solution to the PDE (
8.1
); then the following limit holds:

\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}^{m}-\rho_{t}^{\infty}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=0.

Proof.

Recall that
\rho_{t}^{m}
and the
\rho_{t}^{\infty}
satisfy

\displaystyle\rho^{m}_{t}\,

\displaystyle=e^{t\partial_{xx}}\rho_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{s}^{m})\rho_{s}^{m}]\,ds+\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m},

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{\infty}\,

\displaystyle=e^{t\partial_{xx}}\rho_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}\partial_{x}[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\rho_{s}^{\infty})\rho_{s}^{\infty}]\,ds+\mathcal{Y}_{t},

where
\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
are defined in (
8.8
) and (
8.5
), respectively.
By following the steps that allowed us to prove the limit (
7.7
) and by using Lemma
8.3
we obtain

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{t}^{\infty}-\rho_{t}^{m}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\leq\tilde{c}_{T}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\mathcal{Y}_{t}-\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},

where
\tilde{c}_{T}
a suitable constant depending on
T
.
Hence, to conclude it suffices to prove
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\mathcal{Y}_{t}-\mathcal{Y}_{t}^{m}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
. This follows from Parseval’s identity and (
8.6
). The proof is thus concluded.
∎

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}
be the weighted empirical measure defined in (
2.10
) and let
\rho_{t}
be the solution to (
8.1
).
Then for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the following limit holds

\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\,\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\left|\langle f,\rho_{t}^{N,\varepsilon,M,\kappa,m}\rangle\,-\,\langle f,\rho_{t}^{\infty}\rangle\right|\,=\,0,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.

Proof.

The proof follows by combining Proposition
2.5
and Proposition
8.4
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

Let
\hat{\omega}\in\left(\Omega,\hat{\mathcal{F}},\{\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\hat{\mathbb{P}}\right)
and let be
v_{t}(\hat{\omega})
the corresponding realization of the solution to the SPDE (
1.5
) and let us set
\hat{Y}_{t}^{z}
appearing in (
8.1
) to be equal to the realization of the Brownian motion
w_{t}^{z}(\hat{\omega})
appearing in (
8.2
) for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then, the claim follows by applying Corollary
8.5
to (
8.1
) with the above choice
\{\hat{Y}_{\cdot}^{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
of Hölder paths.
∎

Appendix A
Well-Posedeness results

With the notation set in (
6.5
), and recalling the definition of weak and mild solution given in Section
2
, see e.g. (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), the following holds.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{\cdot}
(as in (
1.1
)) be such that
t\mapsto\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathcal{U})}_{t}
belongs to
C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
and let
\nu_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. Then equation (
1.1
) admits a unique mild solution. In particular this implies that equations (
1.3
), (
1.4
), (
1.5
), (
2.33
) and (
8.1
) are all well-posed in
C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
in mild sense, and, moreover, such mild solutions coincide with weak solutions.

In addition, the (S)PDE (
1.1
) enjoys regularising properties. That is, if
\nu_{t}
is a measure-valued weak solution to (
1.1
) with initial datum
\nu_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
then it
admits a density belonging to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. To be precise,
\nu_{t}
has the same spatial regularity of the convolution
t\to\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}
i.e. if
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}\in H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
) then so does the solution
\nu_{t}
. In particular, weak measure-valued solution of (
2.33
) are also classical solutions (therefore belonging to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
) of such equation.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of a mild solution for (
1.5
) has been proved in
[
2
, Theorem 2.4]
. Since (
1.1
) is a more general version of (
1.5
), and (
1.3
), (
1.4
), (
2.33
) and (
8.1
) are specific instances of (
1.1
), one just needs to prove well-posedness of (
1.1
). This can be done following the steps of
[
2
, proof of Theorem 2.4]
, which goes through as long as the stochastic convolution
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}
in (
6.5
) belongs to
C\left([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)
.
This is true when
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is equal to
W(t,x)
or
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{z}e_{z}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{z}
by Lemma
8.1
and Lemma
8.2
, respectively (giving equations (
1.5
) and (
8.1
), respectively).
It is clearly the case when
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is equal to
q(x)Y_{t}
,
q(x)w_{t}
or
q(x)Y_{t}^{\kappa}
, by Lemma
7.1
(giving equations (
1.3
), (
1.4
), (
2.33
), respectively), since
q
is smooth and
Y_{t}
,
w_{t}
and
Y_{t}^{\kappa}
are all at least Hölder time-continuous (and space-independent). The fact that the weak and mild solutions coincide has been proven in
[
31
, Proposition 3.5]
. We just point out that in the context of
[
31
]
the equivalence between weak and mild solution has been shown for SPDEs satisfying Dirichlet boundary conditions (DBC). In our instance, we are dealing with SPDEs having periodic boundary conditions (PBC). This doesn’t create any additional issue since the proof of
[
31
, Proposition 3.5]
can be applied to our context, without making any modifications, once we have replaced
G(t)
(the heat kernel of the heat equations with DBC) in
[
31
, Proposition 3.5]
with
e^{t\partial_{xx}}
(the heat semi-group of the heat equation with PBC).

To prove the smoothing properties of (
1.1
) we first need to recall what a measure-valued weak solution is. A process
\nu_{t}
is a weak-measure valued solution to (
1.1
) if
t\to\nu_{t}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
(we endow
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
with the total variation norm) and for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the following equality is satisfied: (
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
or
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
a.s. if
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is equal to
q(x)w_{t}
or
W_{t}
, respectively)

(A.1)

\langle f,\nu_{t}\rangle=\langle f,\nu_{0}\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\partial_{xx}f,\nu_{s}\rangle\,ds-\int_{0}^{t}\langle\partial_{x}f,(V+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\nu_{s})\nu_{s}\rangle\,ds+\langle f,\mathcal{U}_{t}\rangle.

Following
[
6
, Proof of Theorem 2]
or
[
26
, Theorem 3.3]
one can see that if
\nu_{t}
is a weak-measure valued solution in the sense of (
A.1
) then it is a
H^{-2}
-valued mild solution (we point out that the converse holds as well i.e. a mild solution is a weak solution. However, we don’t need this here). Namely, the following equality holds in
H^{-2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
15
15

15

In this proof we are using a broader definition of Sobolev space than the one given in (
3.6
). To be precise, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
we define
H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
as the space of distributions
\mathcal{D}
such that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|z|^{2})^{s}\langle e_{z},\mathcal{D}\rangle^{2}<\infty,
where
\{e_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the basis defined in (
6.3
). Let us note that this extension is consistent as
H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
with
s\in\mathbb{N}
coincides with the Sobolev space given in (
3.6
).
:

(A.2)

\nu_{t}=e^{t\partial_{xx}}\nu_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\partial_{xx}}\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+F^{{}^{\prime}}*\nu_{s})\nu_{s}\right]\,ds+\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})},\qquad t\in[0,T].

We also the following regularising property of the heat semi-group (cfr.
[
4
, Lemma 2.3.3]
) which will be used several times. Namely, let
\mathcal{D}\in H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
and let
s_{2}>s_{1}
then

(A.3)

\left|e^{t\partial_{xx}}\mathcal{D}\right|_{H^{s_{2}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{s_{1},s_{2}}t^{-\frac{s_{2}-s_{1}}{2}}\left|\mathcal{D}\right|_{H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.\

This can be proven by using the Fourier representation of
e^{t\partial_{xx}}
combined with the fact that the function
(1+x)^{s_{2}-s_{1}}e^{-tx}\lesssim_{s_{1},s_{2}}t^{-(s_{2}-s_{1})}
, for
x\geq 0
.
Furthermore, we will also need the following estimate of the
H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\nu_{t}
, with
s<-\frac{1}{2}
. Since
\nu_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
then for any given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
we have that

\displaystyle\left|f\nu_{t}\right|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|z|^{2})^{s}\langle e_{z},f\nu_{t}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|z|^{2})^{s}\left[\int_{\mathbb{T}}e_{z}(x)f(x)\nu_{t}(dx)\right]^{2}

(A.4)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|z|^{2})^{s}\frac{1}{\pi}|f|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}|\nu_{t}|_{TV}^{2}

which is finite as long as
s<-\frac{1}{2}
.
Throughout the next calculation we assume that the (stochastic) convolution
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
(the extension to the instance
\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}\in H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
can be done in the exact same way so we don’t repeat it).

Let us now set
s_{1}=-2
and
s_{2}=-1+p
for a small enough
p>0
; then from the mild formulation of
\nu_{t}
we obtain

(A.5)

\displaystyle|\nu_{t}|_{H^{-1+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\lesssim^{\eqref{heat kernel regualrising estimate},\eqref{sitma via total variation}}|\nu_{0}|_{H^{-1+p}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-1+p}\left(\left|\nu_{s}\right|_{TV}+\left|\nu_{s}\right|_{TV}^{2}\right)\,ds+\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}^{(\mathcal{U})}\right|_{H^{-2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.

By combining the above estimate with the fact that
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\nu_{t}|_{TV}<\infty
(this follows since
\nu_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
) and
\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}^{(\mathcal{U})}\in C\left([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)
we obtain
\nu_{.}\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T];H^{-1+p}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)
. From equality (
A.2
) we obtain the continuity in time, i.e.
\nu_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];H^{-1+p}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
.
16
16

16

This property holds in general in any separable Banach space
E
. Namely, if
E
is a separable Banach space and
f\in L^{\infty}([0,T];E)
then its (Bochner) integral
\int_{0}^{t}f_{s}\,ds
,
t\in[0,T]
, (see
[
47
, Appendix A]
) clearly belongs to
C([0,T];E)
.

From this moment onward, one proceeds in a similar fashion. Namely, one now sets
s_{2}=-1+p
and
s_{2}=-2p
for a small enough
p>0
. Then by applying the same procedure that led to (
A.5
) we obtain
\nu_{\cdot}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{-2p}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
which a fortiori implies
\nu_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];H^{-2p}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
. If one repeats the argument with e.g.
s_{2}=0
and
s_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}+p
for a small enough
p>0
then obtains
\nu_{\cdot}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
which shows that
\nu_{t}
admits a density in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. The
H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-regularity of
\nu_{t}
can be now obtained easily. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.

Let us begin with proving the well-posedness of the PDE (
2.16
). The existence of a weak solution to the PDE (
2.16
) follows from the well-posedness of the SDE (
2.13
). Indeed, by a straightforward application of Itô’s formula to the process
t\to\Psi(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
where
\Psi
is a smooth and compactly supported function on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
(i.e.
\Psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
) one can readily see that the joint distribution
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}:=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})
is a weak measure-valued solution to (
2.16
). Therefore, the uniqueness of the solution to (
2.16
) (in the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
) remains to be shown. This can be done by tracing the steps of
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
(let us note that the result in the scenario of
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
is proven for SPDEs and, therefore, it can be a fortiori applied to PDEs).

The coefficients of (
2.16
) do not satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 5.1]
, which are required to prove
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
.

However, upon inspection of
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
one can see that the proof does not hinge on the coefficients
a,b,\sigma
in
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
to satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 5.1]
but rather on their linearised versions
\bar{a},\bar{b}
and
\bar{\sigma}
to satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 4.1]
.

That is, the proof
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
(which takes care of the uniqueness of the solution) works as soon as the linearised coefficients
\bar{a},\bar{b}
and
\bar{\sigma}
in
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
(of
a,b
and
\sigma
, respectively) satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 4.1]
.

Hence, to obtain the uniqueness of the solution to (
2.16
) it suffices to identify the linearised coefficients of the PDE (
2.16
) and verify that
[
11
, Assumptions 4.1]
are satisfied.

To avoid notation conflict we denote by
\bar{D},\bar{B}
and
\bar{\Sigma}
what in
[
11
, Theorem 5.4]
is denoted by
\bar{a},\bar{b}
and
\bar{\sigma}
. In our case, the linearised versions of the coefficients of (
2.16
) are given by

\displaystyle\bar{D}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(t,x,a)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{$t\in[0,T]$, $(x,a)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}$,}

\displaystyle\bar{B}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}(t,x,a)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}V^{{}^{\prime}}(x)+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})\\
-\frac{q(x)}{\Xi_{\varepsilon}(x,\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa})}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{$t\in[0,T]$, $(x,a)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}$,}

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}

\displaystyle\equiv 0.

From (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) applied to
\mu=\nu=\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
one can now readily see that the above coefficients satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 4.1]
, and the uniqueness of the solution to the PDE (
2.16
) follows.

In the case of the PDE (
2.22
) and the PDE (
2.30
) we still make use of the results obtained in
[
11
, Section 4 and Section 5]
but in a different way.

Let us begin with pointing out that contrary to the case of the PDE (
2.16
) where we are dealing with a closed equation for
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
, in the instance of the PDE (
2.22
) one works with the system of two PDEs (
2.22
)-(
2.23
) and proceed as follows. In the first place, we consider the iterative system
17
17

17

In system (
A.6
),
n\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the iteration parameter, there is no
\varepsilon
dependence in such a system; that is,
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
is not to be confused with
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
as it is not
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa}
when
\varepsilon=n
. The sequence
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
is constructed to study (
2.22
)-(
2.23
), not (
2.16
).

(A.6)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa})\right)\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}\right]-\frac{q}{\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}}\partial_{a}\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\\
&\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}=\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}+\partial_{x}\left[\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa})\right)\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}\right]\\
&\mu^{n,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\mu_{0},\quad\zeta^{n,M,\kappa}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0}\end{cases}

with starting condition
\mu_{t}^{0,M,\kappa}=\mu_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
. The linear iterative system (
A.6
) is well-posed once the term
\mu_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa}
from the previous iteration is given: one takes
\mu_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa}
and following the steps in
[
24
, Theorem 3, Theorem 4, Theorem 5 and Theorem 6 in Chapter 7]
solves the second PDE in (
A.6
). This ensures the existence and uniqueness of a classical solution
\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
to the second PDE in (
A.6
). After that, one takes
\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
and solves the system of SDEs (
A.8
). The law of the solution (i.e. the pair
(X_{t}^{n,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{n,M,\kappa})
) to (
A.8
) provides a weak measure-valued solution for the first PDE in (
A.6
). Indeed, if one applies Itô’s formula to the stochastic process
t\to\Psi(X_{t}^{n,M,\kappa},A_{t}^{n,M,\kappa})
with
\Psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and then takes the expectation; the existence of a solution
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
follows.
As for the uniqueness of such
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
, this is a consequence of
[
11
, Theorem 4.8]
. To apply this result, from
[
11
, Theorem 4.8]
we notice that the coefficients of the PDE solved by
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
in (
A.6
) need to satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 4.1]
. In this instance, the coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{n}^{M,\kappa}(t,x,a)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{$t\in[0,T]$, $(x,a)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$}

\displaystyle\bar{B}_{n}^{M,\kappa}(t,x,a)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}V^{{}^{\prime}}(x)+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa})\\
-\frac{q(x)}{\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}(x)}\partial_{t}Y_{t}^{\kappa}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{$t\in[0,T]$, $(x,a)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$}

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}_{n}^{M,\kappa}

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

At this stage, if one now takes into account
(i)
and
(ii)
in Lemma
5.4
applied to
\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
and (
4.7
) applied to
\mu=\nu=\mu_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa}
it follows that for any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
the coefficients
\bar{D}_{n}^{M,\kappa},\bar{B}_{n}^{M,\kappa}
and
\bar{\Sigma}_{n}^{M,\kappa}
satisfy
[
11
, Assumptions 4.1]
and, therefore, the uniqueness of
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}
- weak measure valued solution to the first PDE in (
A.6
)- is obtained from
[
11
, Theorem 4.8]
.

As a byproduct of this we have also obtained that
\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{n},A_{t}^{n})
where
(X_{t}^{n},A_{t}^{n})
is the solution to (
A.8
).

Once this is in place, if we follow the calculations that led to (
A.10
)-(
A.11
) in the proof of Lemma
5.2
one can see that the same inequalities holds for
\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu^{n,M,\kappa},\mu^{n-1,M,\kappa})
and
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{n-1,M,\kappa}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
. Hence, the solution
(\mu_{t}^{n,M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{n,M,\kappa})
induces a contraction in the product space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(C([0,T];\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))
; this a fortiori implies that the solution
(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa})
to (
2.22
)-(
2.23
) obtained beforehand is unique.

The existence and uniqueness of a weak-solution
\mu_{t}^{\kappa}
to the PDE (
2.30
) can be carried out in the same way of the PDE (
2.22
) with
\Gamma_{M}
being replaced by
\Gamma
(defined in (
2.27
)) so we don’t repeat it. The proof is thus concluded.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

In what follows we omit dependence of the quantities at hand on the parameters
\varepsilon,M
and
\kappa
. Hence, we write
X_{t}^{n}
and
\mu_{t}^{n}
in place of
X_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa,n}
and
\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon,M,\kappa,n}
. However, we do keep track of such parameters and in what follows
C(t,\varepsilon,M,\kappa)
denotes a generic constant, depending continuously on
t,\varepsilon,M
and
\kappa
, which may change from line to line.

To prove the well-posedness of system (
2.13
) we apply a standard fixed point type of argument. Let
X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0}
be as in Definition
4.1
and let us set the following iterative scheme:

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{n}=X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\left(V^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{s}^{n})+\Gamma_{M}(X_{s}^{n},\mu_{s}^{n-1})\right)\,ds+\sqrt{2}\beta_{t}\\
&A_{t}^{n}=A_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Xi_{\varepsilon}(X_{s}^{n},\mu_{s}^{n-1})\,dY_{s}^{\kappa}\\
&\mu_{t}^{n}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}^{n},A_{t}^{n}),\end{cases}

where
\mu^{n}|_{t=0}=\mu_{0}
with starting condition
\mu_{t}^{0}=\mu_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
.
Using now (
4.7
) we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}^{n}-X_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(t,M)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}|X_{r}^{n}-X_{r}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)ds\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})\,ds\right],

so that Gronwall’s lemma implies

(A.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}^{n}-X_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)\leq C(t,M)\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})\,ds.

Similarly, from Jensen’s inequality and (
4.8
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|A_{s}^{n}-A_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(t,\varepsilon)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|X_{r}^{n}-X_{r}^{n-1}\right|^{2}\right)|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|^{2}\,ds\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})\,|\partial_{s}Y_{s}^{\kappa}|^{2}ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq C(t,\kappa,\varepsilon)\left[\int_{0}^{t}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\left|X_{r}^{n}-X_{r}^{n-1}\right|^{2}\right)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})ds\right]

for
t\in[0,T]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By combining (
A.7
) and the above inequality we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}^{n}-X_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|A_{s}^{n}-A_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)\leq C(t,M,\kappa,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})ds.

Hence, from the definition 2-Wasserstein with
\mathcal{X}=C_{t}
and inequality (
3.5
) we deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,t}(\mu_{\cdot}^{n},\mu_{\cdot}^{n-1})\leq C(t,M,\kappa,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2,s}(\mu_{\cdot}^{n-1},\mu_{\cdot}^{n-2})\,ds

From this, the statement readily follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

In what follows we omit dependence of the quantities on the parameters
M
and
\kappa
. Hence, we write
X_{t}^{n}
,
\mu_{t}^{n}
and
\zeta_{t}^{n}
in place of
X_{t}^{M,\kappa,n}
,
\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa,n}
and
\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa,n}
. However, we do keep track of such parameters and in what follows
C(t,M,\kappa)
denotes a generic constant, depending continuously on
t,M
and
\kappa
, which may change from line to line.

To prove the well-posedness of system (
2.21
) we apply again a standard fixed point type of argument. Let
X_{0},A_{0},\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
be as in Definition
4.1
and
let us consider the following iterative scheme,

(A.8)

\begin{cases}&X_{t}^{n}=X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}V^{{}^{\prime}}(X_{s}^{n})+\Gamma_{M}(X_{s}^{n},\mu_{s}^{n-1})\,ds+\sqrt{2}\beta_{t},\\
&A_{t}^{n}=A_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{q(X_{s}^{n})}{\zeta_{s}^{n}(X_{s}^{n})}\,dY_{s}^{\kappa},\\
&\partial_{t}\zeta^{n}=\partial_{xx}\zeta^{n}+\partial_{x}\left[(V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{n-1})\zeta^{n}\right],\\
&\zeta^{n}|_{t=0}=\zeta_{0},\end{cases}

where
\mu^{n}|_{t=0}=\mu_{0}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
with starting condition
\mu_{t}^{0}=\mu_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
.
From (
4.7
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}^{n}-X_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}\!\!\right)\!\leq\!C(t,M)\!\left[\!\!\!\,\,\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}|X_{r}^{n}-X_{r}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)\!ds+\!\!\!\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})ds\right],

so that from Gronwall’s lemma we then have

\mathbb{E}\left(\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}^{n}-X_{s}^{n-1}|^{2}\right)\leq C(t,M)\int_{0}^{t}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,s]}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{r}^{n-1},\mu_{r}^{n-2})\,ds,\qquad t\in[0,T].

As for the difference
|A_{t}^{n}-A_{t}^{n-1}|
we follow the steps of (
5.11
) in Proposition
5.3
. Indeed, from
(i)
in Lemma
5.4
applied to
\zeta_{t}^{n}
with
\mu_{t}^{n-1}
we obtain (recall
\eta:=\min\limits_{\mathbb{T}}\zeta_{0}(x)>0
)

(A.9)

\zeta_{t}^{n}(x)\geq\eta\,e^{-t\mathcal{A}(M)},\qquad\text{for every $x\in\mathbb{T}$, $t\in[0,T]$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$}

where
\mathcal{A}(M)
is defined by (
5.4
). Let us point out that from (
A.9
) it follows immediately that for any given
M,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
the function
\zeta_{t}^{n}
is bounded from below uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{N}
and, therefore, also the limit of the sequence will enjoy the same property due to the continuity w.r.t.
x\in\mathbb{T}
of the
\zeta_{t}^{n}
’s.

Once this observation has been made, the rest of the proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
4.2
and makes use of calculations similar to those in (
5.11
), this time using the bounds (
5.3
), (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), so we omit it. From this we obtain

(A.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,t}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{n},\mu_{\cdot}^{n-1})

\displaystyle\leq C(t,M,\kappa)\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2,s}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{n-1},\mu_{\cdot}^{n-2})\,ds,

(A.11)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|\zeta_{s}^{n}-\zeta_{s}^{n-1}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t,M,\kappa)\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2,s}^{2}(\mu_{\cdot}^{n-1},\mu_{\cdot}^{n-2})\,ds,

and this concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

We use exactly the same iterative scheme implemented in the proof of Lemma
5.2
but the function
\Gamma_{M}
in (
2.14
) is replaced by
\Gamma
defined in (
2.27
) and, moreover, also in this proof a lower bound for
\zeta_{t}^{n}
uniform in
n\in\mathbb{N}
is needed (we recall that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the iteration parameter). This is obtained by following the steps and calculations of Lemma
6.6
and
(i)
of Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Appendix B
Lower bound for the solution of linear parabolic PDEs

In this section we provide a lower bound for parabolic PDEs of the following form,

(B.1)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}w=\partial_{xx}w+\partial_{x}[fw],\,\,\quad x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,t\in(0,T]\\
&w|_{t=0}=w_{0},\,\,\quad x\in\mathbb{T}\,,\end{cases}

for the unknown
w=w_{t}(x):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
f\in C\left([0,T];C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)
. We are pretty sure that the bounds we produce here must exists in the literature, but we were not able to find them in the exact form in which we need them, so we present a proof, following the strategy of
[
24
, Theorem 10 in Chapter 7]
. The main result we need is Theorem
B.4
, the other lemmata below are preliminary to that result.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
w_{t}
be the solution to (
B.1
) and
w_{0}\geq 0
then
w_{t}
is non-negative.

Proof.

The proof can be readily adapted from
[
10
, Lemma 2.1 and Corollary 2.2]
, so we don’t repeat it.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
w_{t}
be the solution to (
B.1
) and let
w_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
;
then, for any given
k\in\mathbb{N}
the solution
w_{t}
converges to
w_{0}
in
H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
as
t\downarrow 0
, i.e.

\lim\limits_{t\downarrow 0}|w_{t}-w_{0}|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=0.

Note B.3
.

It is known that for any given
w_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the solution
w_{t}
to (
B.1
) is smooth for positive times (cfr.
[
24
, Theorem 5 and Theorem 6 in Chapter 7]
). With the above lemma and Sobolev’s embedding theorem we extended the smoothness up to the initial time
t=0
, in other words,
w\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

We proceed via an induction argument and we show that for any given
k\in\mathbb{N}
the following two properties hold:

(B.2)

\begin{cases}&|w_{t}|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim_{t,f}|w_{0}|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2},\,\,t\in[0,T]\\
&\lim\limits_{t\downarrow 0}|w_{t}-w_{0}|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=0\,.\end{cases}

If we set
k=0
we have to prove that (
B.2
) holds with
k=0
.
A straightforward calculation shows that for
t\in(0,T]

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{2}w_{t}^{2}\,dx\lesssim_{f}|w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

which implies from Gronwall’s lemma

|w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim_{t,f}|w_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2},\,\,t\in(0,T].

To prove the limit as
t\downarrow 0
a similar calculation is performed. Indeed, by differentiating the function
t\to|w_{t}-w_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
and after some calculations we obtain, for every
t\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|w_{t}-w_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}}w_{t}\partial_{xx}w_{0}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}fw_{t}\partial_{x}w_{0}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{2}w_{t}^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim_{f}|\partial_{x}w_{0}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,.

Integrating with respect to time yields

\displaystyle|w_{t}-w_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim_{f}|\partial_{x}w_{0}|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\int_{0}^{t}|w_{s}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}|w_{s}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\,ds,\,\,t\in(0,T],

which implies

\limsup\limits_{t\downarrow 0}|w_{t}-w_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq 0.

Hence, (
B.2
) for
k=0
is thus proven. With the base of the mathematical induction argument proven, we are left to show the induction step. That is, if we assume that (
B.2
) holds for
k-1
then we have to show that (
B.2
) holds for
k
as well.

The upper bound for
|\partial_{x}^{k}w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}
readily follows from Gronwall’s lemma once one calculates
\frac{d}{dt}|\partial_{x}^{k}w_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
and uses the bound (
B.2
) for
k-1
.

As for the time-continuity in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
of
\partial_{x}^{k}w_{t}
at
t=0
this follows once one calculates
\frac{d}{dt}|\partial_{x}^{k}w-\partial_{x}^{k}w_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
, integrates from
0
to
t
as sets
t\downarrow 0
.
∎

Theorem B.4

(Lower bound for
w
)
.

Let
w
be the solution to (
B.1
) with initial condition
w_{0}>0
on
\mathbb{T}
then the following lower bound holds

(B.3)

w(t,x)\geq w_{0}(x)e^{-t\,a(f)}\,,\qquad\mbox{for every }t\in[0,T],\,\,x\in\mathbb{T},

where
a(f)
is defined as

(B.4)

\displaystyle a(f)=\frac{1}{2}\sup\limits_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}|f|^{2}+\sup\limits_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}f|.

Proof.

We follow closely
[
24
, Theorem 10 in Chapter 7]
.

Step 1)

From Lemma
B.1
we know that
w=w_{t}(x)\geq 0
and therefore, we can consider the function
v=v_{t}(x)
defined as
v=\log(w+\delta)
for any given
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Step 2)
[PDE solved by
v
]
By using the following identities

\displaystyle\partial_{x}v=\frac{\partial_{x}w}{w+\delta},\,\,\,\,\partial_{xx}v+|\partial_{x}v|^{2}=\frac{\partial_{xx}w}{w+\delta}\,,

it can be readily seen that
v
is a solution to the following non-linear PDE

(B.5)

\partial_{t}v=\partial_{xx}v+|\partial_{x}v|^{2}+f\partial_{x}v+\partial_{x}fg_{\delta}(x).

where
g^{\delta}_{t}(x)=\frac{w_{t}(x)}{w_{t}(x)+\delta}
, for every
\delta>0
.

Step 3)
[Differential inequality needed to conclude]
Our aim is to obtain a differential inequality for
v
of the following type

(B.6)

\partial_{t}v\geq-\alpha,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T},\,\,t\in[0,T]

for a suitable constant
\alpha>0
which depends only on the
L^{\infty}\left([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)
-norm of
f
and its derivatives. Indeed, if the above holds, we obtain

\displaystyle v_{t}(x)-v_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\geq-\int_{0}^{1}\alpha\,ds=-t\alpha\,.

By first taking the exponential on both sides and then letting
\delta\downarrow 0
, the above gives (
B.3
).

Step 4)
[Auxiliary function]
In order to get (
B.6
) we begin with noting that by (
B.5
) and Young’s inequality, we have

(B.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v\geq\partial_{xx}v+\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}v|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|f|^{2}-|\partial_{x}f|\left|\frac{w}{w+\delta}\right|\geq\partial_{xx}v-a(f),\,\,

where
a(f)
is defined as in (
B.4
). To obtain the above inequality we have used the fact that
g^{\delta}_{t}(x)\leq 1
, (because
w_{t}(x)\geq 0
).
Let us now consider the function
\ell:[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

\ell=v+t\,\theta

where
\theta
is a positive constant such that
\theta>a(f)
. From Note
B.3
we know that
\ell\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, if we assume that
(t_{0},x_{0})\in(0,T]\times\mathbb{T}
is a minimun point of
\ell
then the following holds at
(t_{0},x_{0})

\begin{cases}&\partial_{x}\ell=\partial_{x}v=0\\
&\partial_{xx}\ell=\partial_{xx}v\geq 0\\
&0\geq\partial_{t}\ell=\partial_{t}v+\theta.\end{cases}

By using (
B.7
) we obtain

\displaystyle 0\geq\partial_{t}\ell=\partial_{t}v+\theta\geq\partial_{xx}v+\theta-a(f)\geq\theta-a(f)

where the above inequality holds at
(t_{0},x_{0})
. Since we have chosen
\theta>a(f)
we get a contradiction. Hence, when
\theta>a(f)
if
(t_{0},x_{0})
is a minimum point then it must lie on the line
\{0\}\times\mathbb{T}
i.e.
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{T}
. Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle v+t\,\theta\geq v_{0},\quad\mbox{for every }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}

which implies (by taking the exponential on both sides)

w_{t}(x)+\delta\geq(w_{0}(x)+\delta)e^{-t\,\theta},\quad\mbox{for every }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T},\,\,\,\theta>a(f).

By letting
\theta\downarrow a(f)
and
\delta\downarrow 0
we obtain the desired result.
∎

Appendix C
The Young integral

In this section we give a quick introduction to the theory of Young integral. For a deeper introduction we refer the reader to
[
42
, Chapter 1]
. Let us begin with defining what a path with finite
p
-variation is.
From this moment onward the couple
(E,|\,\cdot\,|_{E})
will denote a Banach space.

Definition C.1
.

Let
Y:[0,T]\to E
be a trajectory and let
p\geq 1
. We say that
Y
has finite
p
-variation if the following holds

(C.1)

|Y|_{p,T,E}:=\sup\limits_{\mathcal{D}\subset[0,T]}\left[\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r-1}|Y_{t_{i+1}}-Y_{t_{i}}|_{E}^{p}\right]^{\frac{1}{p}}

where the supremum is taken over all partitions of the interval
[0,T]
.

For each
p\geq 1
, we denote by
V_{p}([0,T],E)
or simply
V_{p,T}^{E}
denote the subset of
C([0,T];E)
consisting of those paths which have finite
p
-variation. We endow such a space with the following norm. For each
Y\in V_{p,T}^{E}
, we set

|Y|_{V_{p,T}^{E}}:=|Y|_{p,T,E}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|Y_{t}|_{E}

To avoid any ambiguity, we call
|Y|_{p,T}
the
p
-variation of
Y
on
[0,T]
and

|Y|_{V_{p,T}^{E}}
the
p
-variation norm of
Y
on
[0,T]
. Moreover, the following chain of inclusions holds.

Proposition C.2
.

[
42
, Proposition 1.7]

For each
p\geq 1
, the set
V_{p,T}^{E}
is a linear subspace of
C([0,T],E)
on which
|\,\cdot\,|_{V_{p,T}^{E}}
is a norm. Moreover, the couple
\left(V_{p,T}^{E},|\,\cdot\,|_{V_{p,T}^{E}}\right)
is a Banach space. Finally, if
1\leq p\leq q
, then the following inclusions hold and are continuous

V_{1,T}^{E}\subset V_{p,T}^{E}\subset V_{q,T}^{E}\subset C([0,T];E)

Lemma C.3
.

[
42
, Lemma 1.13]

Let
p
and
q
be such that
1\leq p<q
. Let
X
,
Y
be two paths of
V_{p}^{T}
. Then the following estimate holds

|X-Y|_{V_{q,T}^{E}}\leq\left(2\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|X_{t}-Y_{t}|_{E}\right)^{\frac{q-p}{q}}|X-Y|_{p,T,E}^{\frac{p}{q}}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|X_{t}-Y_{t}|_{E}

Theorem C.4
.

[
42
, Theorem 1.16 and Remark 1.17]

Let
p,q\geq 1
be two real numbers such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}>1

Let
>0
be a positive real number and let us consider two paths

X\in V_{p,T}^{E}
and
Y\in V_{q,T}^{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, for each
t\in[0,T]
, the limit

(C.2)

\int_{0}^{t}X_{s}\,dY_{s}:=\lim\limits_{|\mathcal{D}|\to 0,\,\mathcal{D}\subset[0,t]}\,\,\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}X_{t_{i}}(Y_{t_{i+1}}-Y_{t_{i}})

exists. As a function of
t
, this limit belongs to
V_{q,T}^{E}
and there exists a
constant
C_{p,q}
which depends only on
p
and
q
such that the following inequality holds:

\left|\int_{0}^{\cdot}X_{s}dY_{s}\right|_{E,q,T}\lesssim_{p,q}|X|_{V_{p,T}^{E}}|Y|_{q,T,\mathbb{R}}

Moreover, the following upper bound on the
L^{\infty}
-norm holds

(C.3)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\,\,\int_{0}^{t}X_{s}\,dY_{s}\,\,\right|_{E}\,\lesssim_{p,q}|X|_{V_{p,T}^{E}}|Y|_{q,T,\mathbb{R}}.

In particular, since a path is Hölder continuous with exponent
\alpha
, with
0<\alpha\leq 1
, then it has finite
\frac{1}{\alpha}
-variation we have the following.

Corollary C.5
.

If we let
Y\in C^{\alpha}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
and
X\in C^{\beta}([0,T];E)
then the integral in (
C.2
) is well-defined whenever
\alpha+\beta>1
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Let us start by noting that from Theorem
C.4
and upper bound (
C.3
) we obtain that

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\,\,\mathcal{I}_{f}(t,x)\,\,\right|\,\lesssim_{\gamma}|f_{\cdot}(x)|_{V_{1,T}^{\mathbb{R}}}|Z|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}}.

We aim to obtain an estimate for the
L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\mathcal{I}_{f}
. To this end, we need to estimate the
|\,\cdot\,|_{V_{1,T}^{\mathbb{R}}}
-norm of
f
. By recalling the definition of
V_{1,T}^{\mathbb{R}}
two terms have to be controlled. Namely,
|\,\cdot\,|_{1,T,\mathbb{R}}
and the supremum norm with respect to time
t
. For the supremum norm we simply have:

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}|f_{t}(x)|\leq|f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},\,\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T}.

As for the
1
-variation norm we proceed as follows. If we let
\mathcal{D}
be a partition of
[0,T]
then from the Lipschitz continuity of
f
follows that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}|f_{t_{i+1}}(x)-f_{t_{i}}(x)|\leq\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}|\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|t_{i+1}-t_{i}|\leq T\,\,\,|\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})},\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T}.

Hence by taking the supremum over all partitions of the interval
[0,T]
we obtain that

|f(x)|_{V_{1,T}^{\mathbb{R}}}\leq\max\{T,1\}\left(|f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\right),\,\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T}.

which gives the following upper bound for the
L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\mathcal{I}_{f}

(C.4)

\left|\,\,\mathcal{I}_{f}\,\,\right|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,\lesssim_{\gamma}\max\{T,1\}(|f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})})|Z|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}}.

Furthermore, by applying (
C.4
) to
\mathcal{I}_{\partial_{x}f}
we obtain

(C.5)

\left|\,\,\mathcal{I}_{\partial_{x}f}\,\,\right|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,\lesssim_{\gamma}\max\{T,1\}(|\partial_{x}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+|\partial_{x}\partial_{t}f|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})})|Z|_{\frac{1}{\gamma},T,\mathbb{R}}

By combining (
C.4
) and (
C.5
) we obtain (
7.2
).
We are left to prove (
7.1
). Let us fix a partition
\mathcal{D}
of the interval
[0,T]
and consider the partial sums. Since
f
is smooth both in space and time we have that for any given test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
the following holds

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}f_{t_{i}}(Z_{t_{i+1}}-Z_{t_{i}}),\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}(Z_{t_{i+1}}-Z_{t_{i}})\left\langle f_{t_{i}},\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}(Z_{t_{i+1}}-Z_{t_{i}})\left\langle\partial_{x}f_{t_{i}},\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=-\left\langle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\partial_{x}f_{t_{i}}(Z_{t_{i+1}}-Z_{t_{i}}),\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.

By now letting the mesh size
|\mathcal{D}|\downarrow 0
, by recalling that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}f_{t_{i}}(x)(Z_{t_{i+1}}-Z_{t_{i}})\xrightarrow[|\mathcal{D}|\downarrow 0]{}\mathcal{I}_{f}(t,x),\,\,\,t\in[0,T],\,\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T},

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\partial_{x}f_{t_{i}}(x)(Z_{t_{i+1}}-Z_{t_{i}})\xrightarrow[|\mathcal{D}|\downarrow 0]{}\mathcal{I}_{\partial_{x}f}(t,x),\,\,\,t\in[0,T],\,\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{T}

then from (
C.4
), (
C.5
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem we obtain

\left\langle\mathcal{I}_{f},\partial_{x}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}=-\left\langle\mathcal{I}_{\partial_{x}f},\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}.

which gives (
7.1
) and the proof is thus concluded.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs from Section
4
to Section
8

Proof of
(ii)
and
(iii)
of Lemma
5.4
.

Proof of (ii).

Let us begin with the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
-norm of
\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}=2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\partial_{t}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\,dx=2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\left[\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}+\partial_{x}\left((V^{{}^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}))\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right)\right]\,dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|^{2}\,dx-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}V^{{}^{\prime}}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\,dx-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\,dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|^{2}\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{T}}V^{{}^{\prime}}\partial_{x}\left(\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right)^{2}\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})\partial_{x}\left(\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right)^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})\,dx

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{upper bound bouno}}|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

so that from Gronwall’s lemma we deduce

(D.1)

|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq|\zeta_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}e^{(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})})t},\,\,t\in[0,T].

After having done so, with a similar calculation one can see that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{d^{4}V}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+3|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle M\left|\frac{d^{4}F}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+3M\left|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left|\frac{d^{4}V}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M\left|\frac{d^{4}F}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\right)|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle+3\left(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M\left|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\right)|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

Now, by estimate (
D.1
) and again Gronwall’s lemma, we obtain

(D.2)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|\partial_{x}\zeta_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}e^{(3|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+3M|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})})t}

\displaystyle+\left(\left|\frac{d^{4}V}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M\left|\frac{d^{4}F}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\right)|\zeta_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}e^{(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})})t}

Similarly, for the second derivative

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq|\partial_{xx}(V^{{}^{\prime}}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)})|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|\partial_{xx}(\Gamma_{M}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)})|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3(|V^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2})|\partial_{xx}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle+3(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2})|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle+3(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2})|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

Using (
D.1
), (
D.2
), the above estimate and by again applying Gronwall’s lemma we obtain
(ii)
.

Proof of (iii).

From a straightforward calculation we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t})\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}\right|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\!\!+2\!\!\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t})(\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)})\right|^{2}\!\!\!+\!\left|(\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})-\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t}))\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right|^{2}\!\!dx

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{ineq gammma}}C\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2})|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{stima norma L2 zetamu}}C\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})|\zeta_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}e^{(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})})t}

\displaystyle+(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2})|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

From Gronwall’s lemma we obtain

(D.3)

|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq C|\zeta_{0}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}e^{\left[\left(|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+1\right)+M^{2}\left(|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+1\right)\right]t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\nu_{s})\,ds.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\left|\frac{d^{4}V}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+3|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|(\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})-\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t}))\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t})(\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}-\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)})\right|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|(\partial_{x}\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t})-\partial_{x}\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t}))\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}\right|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\partial_{x}\Gamma_{M}(x,\nu_{t})(\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)})\right|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq^{\eqref{ineq gammma}}C\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})(|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2})+\left|\frac{d^{4}V}{dx^{4}}\right|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}|\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+(3|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}+M^{2}|F^{{}^{\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(T;\mathbb{R})}^{2})|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\mu)}-\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{(\nu)}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

Once this is place, from (
D.1
), (
D.2
), (
D.3
) and again Gronwall’s lemma we obtain
(iii)
.
∎

Proof of
v)
in Lemma
6.8
.

A calculation similar to the one in the proof of
(iii)
in Lemma
5.4
gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq|V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}\,|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\,\,|\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa})-\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})|^{2}|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\,\,|\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})|^{2}|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\,\,|\Gamma_{M}(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})-\Gamma(x,\mu_{t}^{\kappa})|^{2}|\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

By now recalling (
6.11
), (
6.9
), moment estimate (
6.10
) (with
n=2
) and lastly
(ii)
we obtain

\displaystyle|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(t,\eta)\bigg(|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{t}^{\kappa})

\displaystyle+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\bigg)\,,

where we recall
C(t,\eta)
is a constant depending on
t\in[0,T]
and
\eta=\min\zeta_{0}(x)
(there is no dependence on
M\in\mathbb{N}
since the estimates used are uniform in
M\in\mathbb{N}
). From Gronwall’s inequality we have

(D.4)

|\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq C(t,\eta)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\,ds+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\right).

In a similar way, one can see that

(D.5)

|\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{M,\kappa}-\partial_{x}\zeta_{t}^{\kappa}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\bar{C}(t,\eta)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s}^{M,\kappa},\mu_{s}^{\kappa})\,ds+\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}\,\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{|a|>M\}}|a|^{2}\mu_{s}^{\kappa}(dxda)\right),

for
t\in[0,T]
. Combining (
D.4
) and (
D.5
) gives
(v)
and this concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of limit (
8.4
).

We are aiming at proving (
8.4
) under the condition (
8.3
).
Let us begin with denoting by
S_{N}
the following quantity:

S_{N}:=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\,\,\sum\limits_{|z|\geq N}|z|^{2}\lambda_{z}^{2}\left|\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)|z|^{2}}dw_{s}^{z}\right|^{2},\qquad N\in\mathbb{N}.

Clearly,
\{S_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is a non-increasing sequence and, therefore, it admits a limit
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Let be such a limit be denoted by
\bar{S}
. Clearly, we have
\bar{S}\geq 0
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. so that if we showed
\bar{S}=0
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. then (
8.4
) would easily follow. Hence, we turn our attention to proving that
\bar{S}=0
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

By following the steps of the proof of
[
16
, Theorem 2.9]
(cfr.
[
16
, Theorem 2.13]
as well) along with the Factorization lemma
[
16
, Lemma 2.7, pp.20-21]
if we set
m
in
[
16
, Theorem 2.13]
such that
m>\frac{1}{2\delta}
(
\delta
is the parameter appearing in (
8.3
)) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|S_{N}|^{m}

\displaystyle\leq C_{T,m}\left[\,\,\,\sum\limits_{|z|\geq N}|z|^{2}\lambda_{z}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}s^{-2\delta}e^{-2s|z|^{2}}\,ds\right]^{m}

\displaystyle\leq C_{T,m}\left[\,\,\,\sum\limits_{|z|\geq N}\lambda_{z}^{2}|z|^{4\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{-2\delta}e^{-s}\,ds\right]^{m}

\displaystyle\leq C_{T,m,\delta}\left[\,\,\,\sum\limits_{|z|\geq N}\lambda_{z}^{2}|z|^{4\delta}\right]^{m}.

where
C_{T,m,\delta}
is constant positive depending on
T
,
m
and
\delta
.

On the one hand, one can see that from the above computation
|S_{N}|^{m}\leq|S_{0}|^{m}\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
so that from Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem we obtain
\mathbb{E}|S_{N}|^{m}\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\,\,\bar{S}^{m}
. On the other hand, from again the above computation but this time combined with (
8.3
) gives
\mathbb{E}|S_{N}|^{m}\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}0
. The uniqueness of the limit yields
\mathbb{E}\,\,\bar{S}^{m}=0
which implies
\bar{S}=0
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. This concludes the proof.
∎
